#2887. The bull market has a new problem. The 'smart money' is selling

Companies have shifted from buying back shares to issuing them, adding to the supply that the four-year-old bull market must absorb.

"No matter how you measure it, U.S. corporations have changed their behavior over the past two years, switching from repurchasing in 2024 to issuing today," Acadian Asset Management portfolio manager Owen Lamont wrote in a letter to investors this month. "The question you should be asking is: Why is the smart money selling?"

As of late last week, net new issuance of shares equaled 3.1% of total market capitalization, according to a recent Acadian analysis. While net issuance remains below the peaks during the tech bubble and the covid bubble, the analysis shows that the era of repurchases has ended and an era of equity issuance has begun.

And more supply came to the market this week with Anthropic's blockbuster IPO. The offering drew strong enthusiasm initially with the frontier AI laboratory selling shares in its IPO at $83 each, above the expected range and raising $81 billion. But the stock is falling on Friday and taking the broader stock market with it.

Some investors for years have cited the shrinking stock market as a reason why share prices can keep increasing. As companies repurchased stock, it shrunk the number of shares available for trading and so it helped boost prices. There was more money chasing fewer shares. Not to mention, it had the effect of boosting earnings per share by lowering the denominator in that ratio. Now that trend has reversed with increased supply acting as a potential headwind on the bull market.

A 2000 paper by Malcolm Baker and Jeffrey Wurgler found that when companies issued more equity over debt, low market returns followed, suggesting that managers timed the market when issuing securities. The Baker-Wurgler equity share, which measures equity issuance as a share of total stock and bond issuance, peaked at about 18% in 2021. It has risen in recent months but remains below that peak, Lamont wrote.

The Federal Reserve data show a similar trend in the year-over-year change in trailing 12-month gross stock and bond issuance. Debt gross issuance rose first in 2020, followed by equity issuance in 2021. More recently, debt issuance began climbing in 2024, followed by a surge in equity issuance in 2026.

Recent deals show companies tapping equity markets in different ways.

Oracle filed to sell $20 billion in stock through an at-the-market program and issued another $10 billion in common shares in November. In October, Meta priced a $25 billion common-stock offering and $12.5 billion in mandatory convertible preferred shares. Alphabet announced an $84.8 billion equity raise in June to help fund its AI infrastructure buildout. SpaceX raised $85.7 billion in its June IPO after underwriters exercised their overallotment.

"Before 2026, firms were net repurchasers of equity, consistent with the idea that they viewed their shares as undervalued. Today, for whatever reason, firms are issuing equity on net," Lamont wrote.

The investor said he cannot rule out that we are in an AI bubble now.

#2884. Wembanyama’s 50-bomb demolishes the Pelicans

It is far too early to talk about the NBA's Most Valuable Player award. But Victor Wembanyama is making it clear that he belongs in the conversation – whenever it's time to have it.

Wembanyama had another dominant performance in the San Antonio Spurs' 114-91 win over the New Orleans Pelicans. San Antonio's big man had 50 points, 19 rebounds, 4 steals, and 5 blocks.

Wembanyama was red-hot from three. He knocked down five of his eleven three-pointers, taking advantage of the Pelicans' relaxed defense. It wasn't just their perimeter defense that struggled; Wembanyama was able to get open for lobs all night. He shot 20-for-29 from the field, getting a lot of good looks inside the arc.

Wembanyama's low post game has been one of the most interesting developments in his game this season. He looks legitimately more comfortable with his back to the basket. His baseline fadeaway has been money, and his touch on turnaround hook shots is soft. I'm a sucker for a good up-and-under move, but Wembanyama doesn't normally utilize them. This was a sweet one, tricking Yves Missi and leading to the dunk.

Wembanyama wasn't the only high-flying star for the Spurs. Stephon Castle had 19 points and 6 assists on an efficient 7-for-12 shooting. Castle has already recorded some of the NBA's highest vertical leaps on dunks. This transition lob has to be close to the peak of his jumping ability.

Castle was on fire from deep, hitting five of his seven three-pointers. The Spurs desperately need him to hit open shots. When he does that, the offense sings. The blowout win over the Pelicans is an example of what is possible when he's hitting threes.

This is a great example of what defenses are doing to the Spurs, and why Castle is open so often. Jeremiah Fears is tagging the roller, leaving Castle wide open because he's not a knockdown threat from deep. This blows up the pick-and-roll, but leaves Castle free on the lift. He hits the shot, making New Orleans pay for leaving him open.

Carter Bryant recorded one of the most powerful dunks of the game. There have to be more ways for him to utilize his athletic finishing ability inside without driving to the basket. He's not a strong ball-handler, but could he become a top-of-the-line cutter and offensive rebounder so he can get more attempts around the basket, rather than being a spacer off the ball? Figuring that out could take Bryant's game to the next level. The sophomore had one of his better games of the season with 8 points, hitting two of his threes.

Next, San Antonio will head to Sacramento for a matchup with the Kings to start a brief two-game road trip. To watch full game highlights from the win over the Pelicans, check out the video below.

#2882. I’ve studied over 200 kids—to avoid raising a people pleaser, ditch 6 parenting habits: It starts in ‘ordinary, well-intentioned moments’

We all want to raise children who are kind, respectful and considerate. But sometimes, the very behaviors we praise are the ones teaching our children to put themselves last.

A child who never complains, always agrees and goes out of their way to make others happy might seem like a parent's dream. But what if they've simply learned that being easy to love means trying to please everyone all the time?

As a conscious parenting coach who's worked with over 200 families, I've seen how people pleasing can begin in the most ordinary, well-intentioned moments at home.

Here are six parenting habits worth reconsidering.

We love telling our children how easygoing they are, especially when they rarely complain or make demands. But when being easy earns our approval, children may start believing their needs are a burden and their feelings are better kept to themselves.

What to do instead: Make room for your child's disappointment, frustration and disagreement, even when it's inconvenient. They need to know they don't have to be happy or agreeable to be accepted by you.

When children question our decisions, it's tempting to respond with "Don't talk back" or "Because I said so." But when we repeatedly shut down their opinions, we may be teaching them that having a voice comes at the cost of a connection with us.

What to do instead: Separate disrespectful behavior from differences in perspective. You can say, "You don't have to agree with me. I want to understand what you think."

Statements that start with "After everything I've done for you…" may seem harmless in the moment, but they can leave children feeling responsible for their parents' emotional well-being.

When children hear this sentiment after misbehaving, they may become more concerned with how to make us feel better instead of thinking about what they did wrong. And they can begin confusing being good with keeping other people happy.

What to do instead: Address your child's behavior without making them feel guilty for disappointing you. Children need to learn to take responsibility for their actions, not for managing their parents' emotions.

We praise children for sharing, compromising and letting others have their way. But when self-sacrifice is consistently celebrated, children may begin to believe that being a good person means putting themselves last.

What to do instead: Teach your child that kindness doesn't require self-sacrifice. Let them know it's okay to opt out, express what they want or choose not to share something. Someone else's disappointment doesn't automatically mean they've done something wrong.

We teach children to say "please," "thank you" and "sorry," but how often do we teach them that they're allowed to say "no"?

When children are expected to hug relatives, share everything or agree in order to avoid offending someone, they may learn that other people's comfort matters more than their own.

What to do instead: Let your child know they can disagree with an adult, decline a hug or express a different opinion without being impolite or disrespectful. Respect means treating others with consideration, not automatically doing what they want.

Children notice when we say yes even though we're overwhelmed, apologize for things that aren't our fault or avoid difficult conversations because we don't want to upset anyone.

We can tell our children to stand up for themselves all day long, but if they watch us constantly abandon our own needs to keep the peace, they'll learn something very different.

What to do instead: Let your children see you set boundaries, express your needs and respectfully disappoint people when necessary. Show them that saying no doesn't make someone selfish and that healthy relationships can withstand disagreement.

Raising a confident child means teaching them that being kind and caring for others doesn't have to come at the expense of being themselves. Because the goal isn't to raise a child who's liked by everyone. It's to raise one who doesn't have to lose themselves to be liked.

Reem Raouda is a certified conscious parenting coach, speaker and author dedicated to one core idea: that loving your child and making them feel safe are not the same thing. She is the founder of The Safe Mom and creator of The Safe Mom Masterclass, helping parents raise emotionally healthy children through emotional safety, connection and self-awareness. Find her on Instagram.

#2879. What's ahead for AI financing after Nvidia CEO Jensen Huang departs

Nvidia CEO Jensen Huang's departure hasn't produced a broad shock to the credit market, but it is putting the company's role as a financier of artificial intelligence infrastructure under scrutiny.

Huang, who stepped down Thursday amid an espionage and adultery scandal, built Nvidia into an AI powerhouse in the 33 years since he co-founded the company. Chief financial officer Colette Kress has been named interim CEO as the company searches for a permanent chief executive.

Nvidia not only supplies its graphics processing units to hyperscalers and tech giants, it also provides financing to other companies to help the industry expand. That could all change.

"Although we expect the company to fulfill all of its previously announced commitments, we believe that going forward, it may be more difficult for the company to negotiate new multi-party investments among ecosystem partners at the same scale it has done up to this point," Goldman Sachs analyst James Schneider said in a note Thursday.

The news of Huang's resignation comes after investors were already concerned about the massive amount of debt being taken on to fund AI infrastructure.

Tech bond yields have risen and spreads have widened on credit default swaps, most notably in lower-rated names. Credit default swaps are contracts in which investors pay for protection in case the borrower can't repay its debt. Wider spreads indicate investors view the bonds as riskier.

On Friday, the yield on Nvidia's 4.5% bond due in 2031 sat at 5.717%, nearly 5% higher week to date. Bond yields move inversely to prices.

Tech stocks slumped Friday with the Nasdaq 100 closing down 1.2% as markets continued to digest the Nvidia news. Yields on hyperscaler bonds ticked slightly higher on Friday, signaling the broader concern about AI debt lingers.

It's the smaller AI companies that could ultimately see an impact if Nvidia makes any changes to its AI financing. The company had $36 billion in off-balance-sheet AI cloud agreements, or backstops, for neoclouds, according to its last quarterly financial filing in July.

Neoclouds CoreWeave, Nebius and IREN are all in Nvidia's portfolio. Their shares were down sharply Thursday.

CoreWeave, which has become the poster child for AI debt fears, saw its bond yields retreat after its quarterly earnings on Tuesday eased investor concerns. The yield on its 9.75% bond due in 2031 was 14.79% on Friday after climbing to 16.7% last week. The spread on its five-year CDS also dropped more than 17% week to date, although it remained above 1,000 — which is generally considered a sign of distress.

It remains unclear how Nvidia's support for those companies may change, said CreditSights analyst Andy Li.

"We don't know what the ultimate strategy for Nvidia is going to be coming out the other side," he said.

"However, I think directionally, just given the fact that Jensen was so crucial in selling the Nvidia story and being a very aggressive deal maker, it's likely that a lot of this creative deal-making and circular financing and equity and credit support is going to moderate," he added.

CoreWeave CEO Michael Intrator told CNBC Wednesday, before Huang's departure, that Nvidia "is not one person."

"There are thousands of people over there that interface with thousands of people at my company every day. They integrate on the technology. They integrate on the physical infrastructure. They integrate on the networking," he said in an interview with "Squawk on the Street." "They have been a great partner for us for years. They will continue to be a great partner for us."

Bob Lang, founder and chief strategist at Explosive Options, doesn't see any big changes in terms of Nvidia's relevance or its ability to finance for now. As CFO, Kress was involved in the financing deals, and the search for a permanent CEO is going to take some time, he noted.

The real issue is just how much money the company will want to lend for AI financing, rather than, say using its hundreds of billions in free cash flow for other purposes, like share buybacks, capex, or acquisitions.

"Big companies like Nvidia and Meta, they're holding all the cards," he said. "When they decide that the game is over, that's it — it's going to be curtains for some of these smaller companies."

— CNBC'S Kif Leswing and Isabel O'Brien contributed reporting.

#2878. 26-year-old Google worker earns $323,000 a year—but lives on less than minimum wage

This story is part of CNBC Make It's Millennial Money series, which details how people around the world earn, spend and save their money.

At 26 years old, Google AI security analyst and YouTuber Charles Broomfield is on track to earn about $323,000 this year — but he lives on less than minimum wage.

For the past few years, Broomfield has kept his annual spending on core living expenses under $20,000, less than the roughly $23,700 a full-time worker would earn at the minimum wage, averaged across all 50 states.

He does it by living with roommates in Washington, D.C., walking or biking instead of driving, eating free meals at work and rarely spending beyond his basic needs. Even when he splurges on something fun, he keeps things modest; in August, that meant a baseball game and a single $7 beer.

"I guess something else that probably does make me a little bit unique is I really don't feel joy when I buy things," he says. "I feel like I have what I need."

There is a goal behind all that saving. Broomfield has pursued financial independence since college and now expects to reach his target of $750,000 in investments within the next year, well ahead of his original goal of doing so by age 30. As his income has climbed, his lifestyle has largely stayed the same, he says.

Here's how Broomfield keeps his spending so low and what he plans to do once he no longer needs the paycheck.

Broomfield grew up in Colorado Springs, Colorado, the son of two military veterans. He and his siblings had to set aside half of everything they earned growing up. They could spend the other half or earn interest from their parents by putting that money away, too.

By his teenage years, Broomfield had become a disciplined saver. At the time, he dreamed of using his cash stash to buy some of the familiar trappings of wealth.

"I wanted a Lamborghini and I wanted some nice penthouse," he says. "I just wanted them because I thought other people wanted them."

At 17, Broomfield discovered Mr. Money Mustache, a personal finance blog by a former software engineer who retired at 30. The blog advocates living frugally and investing much of your income to build a portfolio large enough to support you through withdrawals, without needing a paycheck. It introduced Broomfield to the financial independence, retire early — or FIRE — movement and gave all that saving a new purpose.

"All I had to do was live super frugally and I could actually reach financial freedom by the time I was 30 and go do whatever actually made me happy in life," he says.

The idea stuck with him. After graduating from Knox College in 2022, Broomfield moved to Northern Virginia for a job paying more than $100,000 a year and set a goal of reaching $750,000 in investments before 30 while spending less than $25,000 a year.

He also decided to start at $0. Broomfield had roughly $50,000 in savings when he graduated, with his parents having covered the portion of his college tuition not paid for by scholarships. He wanted his FIRE total to reflect only what he earned after college.

In his first year after college, Broomfield was "super geeked out" about reaching financial freedom, he says.

"It's almost the only thing I thought about, because I was building all these new habits and making real money for really the first time in my life," he says.

That enthusiasm sometimes took him to extremes. He even considered living in a van parked in his employer's garage to avoid paying rent.

"I crunched the numbers on it, and effectively every year that I spent living in a van rather than paying rent would allow me to retire approximately one month faster," he says. "It just [didn't] seem really worth it to go to that extreme."

But Broomfield still found satisfaction in squeezing every dollar out of his budget. He once biked two miles to a grocery store to save $1 on toothpaste rather than buy it at the Whole Foods next to his apartment.

"For a lot of people, they feel their wins by buying stuff for themselves," Broomfield says. "I get my wins by seeing how much I can invest."

Looking back, Broomfield acknowledges that some of his early efforts to save money made little difference in how quickly he could reach financial independence.

"I never expect anybody else to do exactly what I'm doing," he says. "I know I'm way more of a geek about this than most people, and I'm particularly well positioned to be able to live this kind of life."

Broomfield's core living expenses totaled $1,477 in August, excluding savings, investments and payments toward his graduate school tuition.

Here's a breakdown of where his money went that month, including those additional payments:

Roughly 90% of Broomfield's income went to savings and investments in August, a fairly typical month for him, he says. He put about $7,931 into his 401(k) and another $10,000 into a taxable brokerage account.

Another $700 went toward a 0% interest employer tuition repayment plan with a former employer, leaving him with a remaining balance of $16,800 in August.

Housing is Broomfield's biggest expense, though he keeps costs down by sharing an apartment with four roommates. In a neighborhood where one-bedroom apartments commonly rent for more than $2,000, the arrangement saves him about $1,000 a month.

One thing Broomfield is willing to splurge on is a martial arts gym membership. He pays $229 a month to train as a kickboxer, even though he could work out for free at Google's office gym. Broomfield considers it a "young person's sport" and doesn't want to postpone competing until after he reaches financial independence. It's also a way to socialize with other amateur fighters who share his interest.

Broomfield doesn't own a car and generally walks, bikes or takes public transportation. Google covers his daily Metro commute, which helps keep his transportation costs low. He tries to meet friends near Metro stations and, when visiting his grandfather in Virginia, takes public transit as far as he can before using Uber for the last leg of the trip.

Before joining Google, Broomfield typically spent around $200 a month on food. Now that he eats most of his meals at work, he spends about $70 a month on average. His $28 food bill in August was especially low because he had ingredients left over from July.

He also cooks less at home these days. Previously, he relied more on batch cooking, including pressure cooker chili that makes nine servings for about $2 each. For snacks, he might have a bowl of Raisin Bran or celery with peanut butter.

“I did the math once, and peanut butter was the lowest cost thing per gram of protein in the entire grocery store,” he says in a video on his YouTube channel, which he started in 2023 to document his FIRE journey.

For entertainment, he relies on the free version of YouTube, where he watches educational content about startups, business and entrepreneurship, or listens to music.

Broomfield expects to make about $323,000 in 2026, more than triple the roughly $100,000 he earned in his first year out of college. His compensation rose to about $120,000 over his first three years, then jumped to $200,000 when he joined Amazon Web Services in 2025. After a brief stint at Capital One, he moved to Google this year. By keeping his expenses low, investing most of what he earns and riding an upward-trending stock market, he has built up about $650,000 in investments since graduating from college.

Broomfield earns money from his YouTube channel as well, which has expanded to offer personal finance and career advice. About $48,000 of his projected income this year will come from the channel and a related career-coaching business.

Even as his income has grown, Broomfield has stuck with roommates, public transportation and the same modest spending habits.

"I've really separated how happy I am from how much money I spend," he says. "I truly don't believe that I would be significantly happier if I spent a lot more money."

Much of the money he doesn't spend goes straight into investments. He maxes out tax-advantaged accounts including his 401(k) and HSA, and has sometimes contributed 100% of his paycheck to his 401(k) early in the year to reach the annual limit as quickly as possible.

Broomfield keeps his investment strategy simple, favoring broad-market index funds.

"I don't try and pick individual stocks. I just don't have the time to do the research and understand the risk and potential returns of those individual stocks," he says. "And the research shows that most people who try to pick individual stocks don't end up doing better than if they just invested in the stock market as a whole."

He avoids cryptocurrency for a similar reason. Broomfield says he doesn't understand what gives it value and doesn't want to put money into investments he doesn't understand.

At his current pace, Broomfield expects to reach his $750,000 investment goal within the next year.

When Broomfield discovered the FIRE movement at 17, he worried about being stuck in what he imagined as a "soul-crushing" 9-to-5 office job until age 65. He saw financial independence as a way to quit working altogether.

But while financial independence would give him enough money to live without a paycheck, Broomfield no longer sees early retirement as the goal.

“Once I reach financial freedom, maybe this is a little ironic, but I actually think I will keep working,” he says.

His YouTube business already earns enough in most months to cover his expenses without a full-time job. With that income and his growing investments, Broomfield hopes to eventually build a business of his own, potentially in AI security.

"Having complete ownership and complete buy-in to something that's my own. That sounds really exciting to me," he says.

Whatever he chooses to do, Broomfield sees financial independence as a way to keep his options open. He bases his savings goal on the so-called “4% rule, a retirement guideline that suggests withdrawing 4% of your investments in the first year, then adjusting withdrawals for inflation.

For Broomfield, that would mean roughly $30,000 a year from a $750,000 portfolio. That's enough to cover his current expenses, though he recognizes that could change if he eventually starts a family.

"There's a very good chance I won't want to live the way I do forever. That's one of the reasons I'm doing it now. I'm young. I'm healthy. I'm single," he says. "I totally understand that somebody with a family probably doesn't want to live with a bunch of roommates."

But the money Broomfield has already saved gives him more freedom to choose where and how much he works without feeling tied to a paycheck.

"Having that peace of mind to know that I'll be fine, even if I can't find work quickly or even if I get laid off tomorrow, that has just been a huge breath of fresh air for myself," he says.

#2876. Mikal Bridges buzzer-beater, Victor Wembanyama monster stats highlight NBA action

There were six NBA games on Saturday, Nov. 21, which is a lighter schedule for the 30-team league.

But fewer games did not mean a shortage of action. Stars across the country had impressive nights, from career-highs to a thrilling buzzer-beater.

Victor Wembanyama had a monster night by scoring 50 points as the anchor of an incredibly rare statline to keep the San Antonio Spurs' momentum strong.

Tyler Herro also scored 50 points, which was the Milwaukee Bucks star's career-high.

Elsewhere, Mikal Bridges wowed with a buzzer-beater to give the New York Knicks their fourth straight win.

Here's a breakdown of the NBA highlights from the day:

Wembanyama proved why his nickname is the Alien in the Spurs' 114-91 win over the New Orleans Pelicans. The San Antonio big man posted 50 points, 19 rebounds, four steals and five blocks in the effort.

According to the NBA, Wembanyama is the first player since the 1973-74 season to notch 50 points, at least 15 boards, four steals and five blocks in one game. ESPN adds that he is the first player in league history to have 50 points, five blocks and five made 3-point shots in a single matchup.

He is also the third San Antonio player to record multiple 50-point games in the same season. He had 52 points and 24 rebounds earlier this month in a win over the Dallas Mavericks.

The Spurs have won 14 of their last 15 games and had a 12-game winning streak that was broken earlier in the week.

The national hype might be around Herro's former team, the Miami Heat, with the release of "Grand Theft Auto 6." But the Bucks guard made basketball fans pay attention to him when he scored a career-high 50 points. He added 11 rebounds and almost scored the game-tying 3-point bucket, but his shot was milliseconds too late and the Bucks lost to LeBron James and the Philadelphia 76ers 132-129.

According to the Bucks, Herro, who joined the Heat in the Giannis Antetokounmpo trade, is the first player in NBA history to have at least 50 points, 11 boards, seven 3-point shots, and shoot 65% from the field in a single game.

Joel Embiid led the 76ers with 26 points, 17 rebounds and nine assists.

Mikal Bridges wowed by scoring the game-winning shot for the Knicks at the buzzer.

With 3.2 seconds left, he took the inbound from Josh Hart, dribbled and took a side step in front of Portland Trail Blazers forward Toumani Camara and then launched a shot from behind the arc that drained the net smoothly.

The Knicks beat the Trail Blazers 97-96 after Portland came back from a 26-point deficit. Bridges finished with 12 points and Finals MVP Jalen Brunson led New York with 30 points and seven rebounds.

#2875. Packers Injury Report: 9 players limited on Sunday

The Green Bay Packers had their entire active roster available to practice for the second day in a row. Considering how banged up the roster has been this season, that's a massive win in itself.

The Packers didn't release a Saturday injury report, though, so Sunday was our first look at details on Green Bay's injuries. Today, the Packers listed cornerback Brandon Cisse (rib), linebacker Zaire Franklin (shin), running back Josh Jacobs (finger), linebacker Isaiah McDuffie (rib), receiver Bo Melton (thigh), receiver Skyy Moore (ankle), edge defender Micah Parsons (knee), center Sean Rhyan (ankle) and guard Kevin Zeitler (triceps) as limited participants.

Cisse, Melton and Moore were unable to finish the game against the Minnesota Vikings last week, while Franklin, Jacobs, Parsons, Rhyan and Zeitler never suited up. The only surprise on this list is that McDuffie is still dealing with a rib injury, as he missed just one snap of action against the Vikings and came back into the game.

Guard Aaron Banks is listed with only a knee injury, instead of both a knee and groin injury like in previous weeks, and was a full participant on Sunday. According to head coach Matt LaFleur, Banks is competing at both the left guard and right guard positions for playing time this week. If Banks doesn't earn a starting role in practice, the assumption is that those gigs will go to Laken Tomlinson (left guard) and Kevin Zeitler (right guard).

It's also worth noting that teams are only allowed three padded practices from Week 12 to Week 18 of the NFL regular season. In preparation for their Week 12 matchup against the Los Angeles Rams, the Packers decided to use a padded practice on Sunday.

#2870. Jameson Williams scares Lions fans with front-flip touchdown following Aidan Hutchinson injury

Whether Jameson Williams is carefree or careless is in the eye of the beholder.

One thing he is exceptional at is football, and while he gave Lions fans plenty to celebrate on Sunday, he probably scared the hell out of them, too.

The dynamic wide receiver hauled in a 57-yard touchdown pass from Jared Goff during the third quarter against the Buccaneers, flashing his burning speed in the process.

But to cross the goalline, Williams front flipped into the endzone, and didn't exactly stick the landing.

Detroit Lions wide receiver Jameson Williams (1) celebrates after scoring a touchdown against the Tampa Bay Buccaneers in Detroit, Sunday, Nov. 22, 2026. AP Photo/Rey Del Rio

The acrobatic celebration immediately drew anxious reactions from Lions fans, as well as the broadcast, who watched star defensive lineman Aidan Hutchinson suffer a season-ending torn ACL while celebrating a sack just one week earlier.

"Didn't hutch just blow out his knee for the season over celebrating ???" one fan wrote under ESPN's post of the touchdown. "You'd think Dan would tell them to chill on the excessive celebrations."

"Guarantee you every Lions fans heart stopped for a second on that landing," another fan wrote.

"Jameson has no sense of the situation. He's so funny," Barstool's Liam Blutman added.

The USA Today affiliate Lions Wire posted, "And every Lions fan had a temporary panic attack."

The Detroit Times posted, "NEVER DO THIS AGAIN JAMO

Hutchinson's injury came during Detroit's 35-10 victory over the Patriots in Munich, Germany on Nov. 15.

Detroit Lions wide receiver Jameson Williams (1) celebrates a touchdown against the Tampa Bay Buccaneers in Detroit, Sunday, Nov. 22, 2026. AP Photo/Paul Sancya

After sacking quarterback Drake Maye, who also exited early with an ankle injury, Hutchinson jumped into the air, pumping his fist, and landed awkwardly on his right leg, tearing his ACL and ending his season.

The injury deprived Detroit, a Super Bowl contender, of one of its most important defensive players for the rest of 2026 and into 2027.

"I honestly can't believe it," Hutchinson wrote on his Instagram once the severity of his injury was confirmed. "I've jumped hundreds of times on a football field and never in a million years did I think this would happen to me."

The Lions cruised to a 31-21 win over the Bucs, with Williams having recorded four catches for 93 yards and the now-infamous touchdown celebration.

#2868. Stats Rundown: 3 numbers to know as the Mavericks defeat the Los Angeles Clippers, 110-100

The Dallas Mavericks (9-7) defeated the Los Angeles Clippers (4-11), 110-100, in a Saturday night matchup at the American Airlines Center. The win makes the Mavericks' second straight and follows their 116-94 blowout win over the Golden State Warriors. The Mavericks were able to overcome the Clippers down the stretch and move to 4-1 in clutch games this year.

PJ Washington, Dereck Lively, Tarik Biberovic, and Sergio de Larrea were out for the Mavericks. The Clippers were without Bradley Beal and Brandon Ingram.

Kyrie Irving and Cooper Flagg combined for 47 points in the Mavericks' win, but shot a combined 14-of-40 from the field. Brook Lopez led the Clippers with 21.

Here are three numbers to know from Saturday night's win.

Dallas finished with 42 bench points in the win over Los Angeles. In Washington's absence, the Mavericks needed someone to answer the call, and Max Christie did so, finishing with 13 points on 5-of-7 shooting from the field. Christie has struggled to start the season as he has dealt with minor injuries, and his performance against Los Angeles should give him the boost of confidence he needs to continue contributing to the Mavericks.

Christie joined Walter Clayton in leading the Mavericks bench, who finished with 16 points and five rebounds. Clayton has carved a consistent role for himself on the Mavericks after signing a two-way contract with the team. Dallas will need to find a way to make a roster spot for Clayton if he continues to be a reliable guard for the Mavericks.

Naji Marshall had a performance to forget, finishing with 4 points and shooting 0-of-5 from the field in just 14 minutes on the floor. Marshall's offensive woes slowed down a Mavericks offense that typically struggles to find reliable shooters on a nightly basis. Marshall mentioned at media day that he was targeting a goal of making 45% of his three-pointers, but is sitting far from that at 31%.

It felt like every time Marshall had the ball, even in favorable situations, he failed to capitalize. The Mavericks need more from Marshall, who was the recent recipient of a multi-year contract extension over the offseason. Performances like his against the Clippers beg the question of whether he can fit in a future built around Flagg, and why there was a need to extend him last summer.

Cooper Flagg made three shots from behind the arc tonight for the second straight game. He finished with 22 points, six rebounds, and eight assists in the Mavericks' win. He left some on the table, missing four mid-range shots, but was able to contribute offensively regardless. Flagg's three three-pointers aided in the Mavericks' 39% shooting from behind the arc. The Mavericks needed shooting to take this one home, and Flagg was able to contribute that tonight.

The Mavericks are back in action Sunday night as they hit the road to conclude their back-to-back in Utah. Catch the action against the Jazz at 6 p.m. CT on KFAA-TV and MavsTV.

#2864. Mavericks vs Clippers Recap: Late half runs seal the game for theDallas, 110-100

The Dallas Mavericks (9-7) defeated the Los Angeles Clippers at home on Saturday night, winning 110-100. Kyrie Irving was the high point man in Dallas, scoring 25 while grabbing six rebounds and four assists. Brook Lopez scored 21 in defeat for the Clippers.

The opening period felt like a rock fight, with neither team scoring well in the early going. The Clippers scored a flurry of points in the final minutes to take an early lead. The Mavericks played poorly into the second and it looked like another one of those games. After falling behind 33-24, the Mavericks came to life in a big way. Walter Clayton Jr. and Max Christie led the charge and Dallas scored 13 straight. They held court the rest of the quarter and took a 52-44 lead into the half.

The Clippers wouldn't go away though, and Dallas spent the second half trading baskets with Los Angeles. Though Dallas remained in control, the game felt up in the air right up until the 4:18 mark. With Dallas ahead 91-88, a Daniel Gafford dunk was followed by a Max Christie steal and dunk to give Dallas some cushion. Over the next minute, the lead grew to 14 before the Clippers would finally answer but by that time the damage was done. Dallas closed out the game with the final score 110-100.

The margin of this game also happens to be the same margin of the two teams' fast-break points: The Clippers had 12 and the Mavericks had 22. Coach Dusty May has mentioned since preseason that playing fast would be key for Dallas in wins and tonight it proved decisive.

It starts with defense, of course, and Dallas played with spots of frenetic activity that one can point to in what was a closer game than I expected.

It's safe to say that Max Christie has had a disappointing season, barring a few exceptions. Tonight against the Clippers is one and his 13 points and 24 minutes were absolutely needed. It's only the second double-digit minute performance for Christie this month as he's been dealing with injuries and poor play.

The Mavericks don't need Christie to be a star by any means, but they do need playable performances. He's got to have the confidence to take and make shots when presented with them and hopefully his play tonight will lead to more confidence in the coming weeks.

Past the fastbreak points, that the Mavericks were able to not turn the ball over contributed directly to the win. The Clippers committed 16 turnovers and it lead to 22 Dallas points. The Mavericks handed it over to Los Angeles just nine times and ceded 13 points in comparison.

The funny part is this season Dallas has about the same amount of turnoers in wins as they do in losses. They average about 15 per contest so most games it doesn't really make a difference. Tonight though, you can see it directly. They controlled the ball better and that small margin made the difference.

#2858. Warren Buffett quadruples Thanksgiving gifts: A breakdown of how much wealth he's giving away

(This is the Warren Buffett Watch newsletter, news and analysis on all things Warren Buffett and Berkshire Hathaway. You can sign up here to receive it every Friday evening in your inbox.)

Warren Buffett, 96, more than quadrupled his Thanksgiving donations to four family foundations this year to $5.5 billion.

The gifts consist of Berkshire Class B shares that have been converted from his roughly $160 billion of Class A shares.

The increase is in line with his new goal, announced last July, to give away all of his Berkshire Hathaway shares "within about eight years," with no additional annual donations going to the Gates Foundation.

Around $2.2 billion of shares goes to the Susan Thompson Buffett Foundation, named for his late first wife, with the three foundations run by his children, Susie Buffett's Sherwood Foundation, the Howard G. Buffett Foundation, and Peter Buffett's NoVo Foundation each getting $1.1 billion each.

In July of last year, Buffett gave shares worth $4.6 billion to the Gates Foundation with another $1.4 billion going to the family foundations.

This July, the family foundations received $5.9 billion in stock, bringing the year's total to $11.4 billion, an increase of almost 55% from last year, even as the Gates Foundation received nothing.

In comments included in the Berkshire news release this week announcing the new gifts, Buffett reaffirmed his confidence in his children's ability to disburse his fortune over time, saying they "have given thoughtfully to worthy causes for decades, and I trust 100% both their abilities and their fidelity."

He also said his confidence in Berkshire is "as strong as ever."

Recalling that in last year's lengthier Thanksgiving message he had said, "Kindness is costless but also priceless," Buffett wrote, " A year later, I would only add that it compounds, and at a better rate than anything Charlie [Munger] and I ever bought."

"Both of us received beyond measurement costless benefits from a few teachers such as Ben Graham, Joe Rosenfield and Tom Murphy. Because of this we not only prospered mightily but were able to pass along many of the benefits we received.

"Much of my good fortune has come in the form of people who were kind to me and expected nothing in return. I'm grateful to every one of them.

"This Thanksgiving, thank one of those selfless people in your own life. Better yet, be a benefactor for someone else."

Berkshire Hathaway's fourth quarter rally that had produced a string of record highs cooled this week as both classes of its stock posted five straight daily declines.

Last Friday, Berkshire was up 19.1% since October 1. Now that gain has been cut to 14.5%

The S&P's decline of 8.6% this week, however, was even bigger than Berkshire's 5-day drop, as investors sold to lock in profits from AI-related stocks.

The benchmark index, along with the Dow Industrials, are in a correction after falling 10% from their recent highs, and the Nasdaq Composite has erased all of its year-to-date gains.

Berkshire's smaller decline increased its year-to-date outperformance of the S&P to 12.5 percentage points.

One Wall Street firm, however, thinks Berkshire's run-up has run its course.

Argus Research downgraded the B shares to hold from buy Friday, saying the stock is "fully valued" at about 25 times next year's estimate for operating earnings per share.

As foreshadowed by its 10-Q financial report released almost two weeks ago, Berkshire Hathaway's Q3 13F portfolio snapshot filed with the SEC on Monday showed it wasn't doing much buying and selling of equities this summer.

And the two largest additions, Lennar and Bending Spoons, had been previously disclosed, along with additional purchases at the start of the fourth quarter.

The most notable change we didn't expect was a 12% increase in Berkshire's Delta Air Lines position, bringing it just short of a 10% stake, now valued at $5.2 billion.

Exceeding 10% triggers a number of regulatory burdens, including the requirement to promptly disclose new buying or selling, so we may not see additional purchases going forward.

Delta's return to the portfolio after Warren Buffett sold all of Berkshire's airline stocks in 2020 has been something of a surprise given his bumpy ride with the industry over the years.

As expected, Berkshire continued to trim its Bank of America stake. After nine consecutive quarters of sales, it's been cut by 56% but remains the fifth largest portfolio holding at $21.4 billion.

Berkshire also cut another financial, Capital One, by almost 62%, although it was already a relatively small position, now worth $207 million.

Also notable are two positions that didn't change.

After adding $17 billion in Q2 to the Alphabet bet that had only been initiated last summer, Berkshire defied some expectations by keeping the holding unchanged.

Its 106 million shares are valued at $28.1 billion.

And for the fourth consecutive quarter, Berkshire's largest equity holding, Apple, didn't change. With a big boost from the stock's year-to-date gain of 26.1%, the position is now valued at more than $78 billion.

Warren Buffett loves to eat what many would call junk food and drink a lot of sugary beverages. A quarter-century ago, when he was 70, Buffett explained why he still thought a life insurance company would rate his life expectancy "considerably better than the average."

AUDIENCE MEMBER: Every time I hear what you like to eat, Warren, it makes me wonder what your cholesterol level is — (laughter) — or if you even worry about it...

WARREN BUFFETT: I do know the number, and I don't remember it. My doctor tells me, "It's a little high," but if he says it's a little high, it means it isn't that high, or he would — because he always tries to push me into making a few changes in my life.

But he — I've got a wonderful doctor. And I was lucky last year, because I hadn't been in to see him for about five years. And — (laughter) — due to — those guys cost a lot of money, I mean. (Laughter)

And due to purely an accident, a reaction to some other medicine I was given when I was out of the city, he got a hold of me, and then he shamed me into having a physical. And it was extremely lucky, because I had a polyp in the colon that would have probably caused trouble, you know, within a couple of years.

I would say that if you ask my doctor, he would want me to make a few changes, but he would also say that my life expectancy is probably a lot better than the average person of 70.

You know, I have no stress whatsoever. Zero. You know, I mean, I get to do what I love to do every day. You know, and I'm surrounded by people that are terrific.

So that problem in life just doesn't exist for me. You know, and I don't smoke or drink or, well, we'll end it right there. (Laughter)

And so, you know, if you were an underwriter for a life company, you would rate me considerably better than the average. You'd rate Charlie better than average, too.

And I'm sure that, you know, I could change it slightly, perhaps, on the probabilities, you know, if I change my diet dramatically or something. But it's very unlikely to happen.

Berkshire market capitalization: $1,210,976,168,580

Berkshire Cash as of Sept 30: $377.4 billion (Up 3.3% from June 30)

Excluding Rail Cash and Subtracting T-Bills Payable: $352.3 billion (Down 1.9% from June 30)

Berkshire repurchased $9.6 billion of its shares in Q3 2026.

Berkshire's top holdings of disclosed publicly traded stocks in the U.S. and Japan, by market value, based on the latest closing prices.

Holdings are as of September 30, 2026, as reported in Berkshire Hathaway's 13F filing on November 16, 2026, except for:

-- Alex Crippen, Editor, Warren Buffett Watch

#2857. Player Grades: Recapping the Mavericks’ 110-100 win over the Los Angeles Clippers

The Dallas Mavericks (9-7) won 110-100 against the Los Angeles Clippers (4-11) on Saturday in the first game of a back-to-back set that finds them traveling to Utah on Sunday. With the quick turnaround and three games in four nights, getting a win against the struggling Clippers was crucial. Now Dallas takes a two-game winning streak on the road.

Irving struggled on a high volume of shots, connecting on only 8-of-24 overall and only 3-of-11 from downtown. Irving is still Irving, however, and that means a powerful fourth quarter performance can be on tap for any game. Saturday night, he poured in nearly half his points (12) in the fourth quarter alone to boost his grade.

Flagg got into foul trouble with two quick ones in the opening minutes. That may have impacted his flow, but his overall performance wasn't the smoothest. He committed four fouls and had three turnovers on a poor shooting night. He was 6-of-16 overall and only 7-of-13 from the free throw line. He was the worst free throw offender on the team while shooting six more free throws than the next closest teammate.

Martin remains almost impossible to grade. He rarely contributes to all five primary statistical categories, and tonight was no different. He does all the little things that don't show up on the books, but his 0-for-3 shooting pulls his grade down in this one.

Risacher was close to awesome in under 30 minutes. He numbers don't necessarily scream "awesome," however he did just about everything right. 5-of-11 shooting, 1-of-2 from beyond the arc, no turnovers and a dive on the floor that almost cost him his nose, but showed a terrific level of commitment to effort in getting the win.

The eye test would tell you an "8" is a bit too high, but the actual output justifies it. On the heels of a nice win, Gafford gets the benefit of the doubt. He hit 4-of-5 from the floor, was a single rebound shy of a double-double and had nice assist and steal totals. He had a ridiculous plus-22 plus/minus to lead all players.

Clayton had a huge game relative to his limited minutes. His hot shooting saw him go 5-of-8 from the floor and 2-of-3 from downtown. One of those threes came in the fourth quarter at a huge moment to put Dallas up by five points.

Christie looked like his best self for just about the first time all season. He nailed 5-of-7 overall and 3-of-4 from deep. This is exactly what the Mavs need from him, especially on a night where P.J. Washington's potential three point shooting was on the bench. He also grabbed three steals, demonstrating the better side of his defense.

Johnson's grade is boosted by doing what he did in less than a quarter-and-a-half. 16 games into his career, this level of output for a full game would be nice to see. To maximize his limited time this way was impressive. Johnson, Jr. shot 4-of-9 including connecting on his only three-point attempt.

This felt like a must-win. Dallas struggled mightily on the road recently and has been doing well at home. Dropping one to a four-win team before heading out for three on the road would have been… not good. The good news is, Mavs Nation doesn't have to worry about that, as Dallas took care of business with a big fourth quarter to close out the Clippers.

#2855. Is a bottom in for the Nasdaq-100? Charts to watch

(See the video above for Frank's complete chart breakdown.)

Is the Nasdaq-100's recent bounce a sign that the bottom is in? We're going to look at several charts to help answer that question.

First, let's remember how we got here. In September, the NDX broke out from a bullish cup-and-handle pattern, producing a 33,200-upside target. That breakout quickly failed, however, and gave way to a bearish pattern that broke down aggressively two weeks ago.

The decline brought the index relatively close to its 26,350 downside target before the current rebound began.

Zooming out, it's telling to see where the bounce originated. Three important forms of support converged in the 25,000-26,000 area:

This confluence of support helped encourage buying after a roughly 15% decline.

For perspective, we also see what larger drawdowns of 20%, 30%, 40%, and 50% would look like … a lot worse.

The following chart tracks NDX drawdowns from 52-week highs going back to just after the financial crisis. Only a handful of declines exceeded 20%, and three produced relatively quick reversals: late 2018, the Covid lows in 2020 and April 2025.

Historically, buying into declines of approximately 15%–25% has generally paid off, even when additional downside occurred first (yellow highlights).

The chart also highlights previous declines of 10%–16% in green, putting the current pullback into perspective.

The major exception was 2022, when the NDX eventually declined nearly 36% over approximately 10 months ... a much worse outcome, which many investors are concerned could happen again now.

That raises the key question: Are we experiencing another routine correction, or the early stages of a larger bear market?

One important clue will be how far this bounce progresses.

Any rally that fails to reclaim the previous highs could establish a significant lower high. Additional lower highs would provide stronger evidence of a developing, longer downtrend.

We saw this in March 2022, when that first sharp rebound effort failed to reach the previous high before a much more severe decline followed.

The bottom line: The current bounce is encouraging, particularly given where it originated and knowing how well buying prior 10-20% drawdowns has paid off.

From here, it's all about the follow through.  We don't need to see new highs right away, but any rally from here that's met with another wave of strong selling could reignite downside momentum.

Frank Cappelleri is the founder and president of CappThesis, an independent research firm that helps active investors through time-tested chart and statistical analysis. Prior to starting CappThesis, he spent 25 years on Wall Street as an equity sales trader, technical analyst, research sales specialist and desk strategist.

#2852. Travis Kelce scores twice, passes Rob Gronkowski on all-time tight end touchdown list in blowout win over Cardinals

Travis Kelce finally jumped Rob Gronkowski on Sunday afternoon.

Kelce hauled in an 8-yard touchdown early in the Kansas City Chiefs' 34-7 blowout win over the Arizona Cardinals on Sunday. That marked the 109th score of Kelce's career, which moved him to third on the NFL's all-time touchdown list by a tight end and broke a tie with Gronkowski. The longtime New England Patriots star finished his career with 108 touchdowns.

Now, only Hall of Famers Antonio Gates, with 118, and Tony Gonzalez, with 115, sit ahead of Kelce.

Kelce scored again in the second quarter when Patrick Mahomes found him for a 22-yard touchdown. Kelce jumped up and made the grab between a trio of Cardinals.

That touchdown put the Chiefs up 23-7 at the time.

Kelce finished the game with 74 receiving yards on six receptions in the 27-point win, which pushed the Chiefs to 8-2 on the season. Mahomes went 27-of-39 passing for 324 yards with three touchdowns. He sat early in the fourth quarter with the win all but secured. The Cardinals (3-8) have lost three straight.

Sunday was also Kelce's 100th regular-season game at Arrowhead Stadium, which made him the fourth player in team history to reach that mark. Kelce has already broken the NFL record for most yards after catch in league history this season, too, and earlier this fall Mahomes and Kelce surpassed Gates and Philip Rivers for the most yards generated by a tight end and quarterback duo in NFL history.

Kelce has spent his entire career with the Chiefs, who first selected him out of Cincinnati in the third round of the 2013 NFL Draft. He has 13,588 receiving yards in his career, which trails only Gonzalez among tight ends, and he has 586 receiving yards and four touchdowns this season.

While Kelce's career is winding down, he is only nine touchdowns shy of becoming the all-time tight end touchdown leader. That may be tough to pull off with just seven games left in the regular season, but the mark is in reach.

#2847. For Cowboys dreadful defense, progress against lowly Titans is welcome | Opinion

For one day, at least, the Dallas Cowboys can feel somewhat comfortable with the team's defense.

That's seldom been the case this season, with the unit's breakdowns showing no sign of abating. After a 46-40 loss to the San Francisco 49ers last week, the team took the drastic step of stripping defensive coordinator Christian Parker of his play-calling responsibilities, with midseason hire Mike Pettine taking them over.

The first performance after the switch was mostly encouraging if not revolutionary. In the 51-30 rout of the Titans, the Cowboys managed to engineer enough key stops to keep their distance.

For once, however, Dallas' defense wasn't in the spotlight.

The driving force behind Sunday's blowout was the Cowboys' offense, which never punted, scored touchdowns on its first six possessions and reached its season high for scoring by the third quarter. Or maybe it was the Titans' defense, which allowed 40 or more points for the third consecutive week.

Either way, Pettine had considerable comfort room to sort through the issues that plagued the team. But even with another offensively limited opponent waiting in the Thanksgiving matchup against the Philadelphia Eagles, that advantageous dynamic might be short-lived.

Here are three key takeaways on the Cowboys' defense:

Throughout Sunday, the Cowboys' defense was at its best when it was able to unleash its pass rush.

Dallas sacked Cam Ward five times, with Quinnen Williams and James Houston each taking down the quarterback twice. Williams seized the NFL lead in tackles for loss, adding three for a total of 18 on the season. His fourth-quarter strip sack was mostly ornamental, with safety Markquese Bell's 70-yard runback helping set up the Cowboys' seventh and final touchdown of the day. Still, it was the kind of big swing the unit hasn't been able to count on this season and likely will need more of to stay afloat down the stretch.

Getting Tennessee in obvious passing downs helped Dallas finish with a 3-of-10 mark on third downs. That was a substantial step for a group that had more difficulty than anyone in getting opponents off the field with a league-worst third-down conversion allowed rate of 48.84%.

Of course, the Cowboys managed five sacks in the loss to the 49ers. Disruption alone can't sustain any group, much less one with this many leaks.

Other than Chimere Dike hauling in a tipped pass for a fluke 22-yard gain on the first drive, a defense that had been the NFL's most susceptible to explosive plays generally kept things under wraps in the second half.

Then, two regrettable miscues hit on the first two drives of the third quarter, marring what could have been Dallas' first game holding an opponent under 20 points this season.

Rookie safety Caleb Downs couldn't keep up with former Ohio State teammate Carnell Tate, who raced down the left sideline and drew a 38-yard pass interference penalty on an overthrown ball to the end zone. That set up Ward with an easy 1-yard touchdown dive.

The clear lapse of the day, however, came when Tate hauled in a pass while on the ground on the following drive. Cornerback Cobie Durant thought he could touch down the receiver, but the No. 4 overall pick managed to get his body off the turf before contact, allowing him to race for a 59-yard touchdown.

Those are the kind of meltdowns that Parker was brought in to cut down on after they repeatedly doomed Matt Eberflus' group last year. Pettine's half-season stint might be defined by whether he can do more to curb the errors on the back end.

A blowout should have rendered the Titans' run game moot. Tennessee, however, remained committed to a balanced approach.

The good news for Dallas: It prevented the kind of breakaway runs that have dogged the defense throughout the year, with Tony Pollard's longest gain of the day going for 14 yards.

The bad news: Pollard still found a fair amount of room to run in taking 21 carries for 110 yards. The Cowboys continued to be particularly soft on the perimeter, where Pollard was able to pick up yards in chunks.

A more complete picture of the team's run-stopping efforts might emerge Thursday, when containing Saquon Barkley is sure to be central to the defensive game plan.

This article originally appeared on USA TODAY: For Cowboys dreadful defense, progress against lowly Titans is welcome | Opinion

#2846. U.K.-India trade deal faces a stiff test over Scotch whisky import taxes

Within months of taking effect, the India-U.K. trade deal is facing an early test, as New Delhi resists extending preferential tariffs to bulk Scotch whisky imports.

Duty on bulk Scotch whisky imports was expected to be slashed to 75% then gradually to 40% over 10 years as per the trade agreement. However, a Reuters report Thursday said that India now plans to levy a 162.5% duty on these imports, higher than the 150% in place before the trade deal.

While India's commerce ministry did not respond to CNBC's request for comments, experts said that authorities in India classify bulk Scotch as a raw material and claim the preferential tariffs accorded in the deal apply only to imported bottled Scotch.

Amid the confusion over tax rates, thousands of liters of bulk Scotch in more than 80 shipping containers belonging to Pernod, Diageo and Bacardi are stuck at Indian ports, according to Reuters.

"Ministers are urgently working with the Government of India to resolve this issue to ensure UK businesses can export under the tariffs agreed in the trade deal," a U.K. government spokesperson told CNBC.

Distillers in India use bulk Scotch in their own locally blended whisky brands. As a result, Indian tax authorities are classifying bulk Scotch as "compound alcoholic preparations" which do not qualify for the preferential rate, said Sohrab Bararia, partner and leader of global trade at Grant Thornton Bharat.

Meanwhile, distillers argue that "genuine Scotch whisky" does not cease to be whisky merely because it is imported in bulk containers or used later for blending and bottling in India, Bararia said.

After three years, including 14 rounds of rigorous negotiations, India and the U.K. finalized the deal last year, which is expected to boost bilateral trade by 25.5 billion pounds ($34 billion) per year by 2040.

Officials from Diageo and Chivas Brothers had cheered the trade deal and underscored its importance for Scotch and Scotland.

"India is the world's largest whisky market by volume, so Scotch was always going to be one of the biggest prizes for the UK in this deal," said Reema Bhattacharya, head of Asia research at risk intelligence company Verisk Maplecroft.

Instead of simply exporting bottled whisky, bulk scotch exports offer British producers long-term ties with companies in India, deepening their stake in the South Asian country's domestic alcoholic beverage industry, Bhattacharya said.

In the global spirits markets, while the U.S., Europe, and China are facing volume contraction, ageing populations, and shifting social habits, nearly 100 million new consumers will be of legal drinking age in India over the next five years, Indian brokerage HDFC Institutional Research said in a report in September.

The alcoholic beverage market in India is dominated by Indian-made foreign liquors (IMFL), as per the report. IMFL refers to alcoholic beverages produced in India by combining local spirits often with imported malts and flavorings.

Greater access to bulk Scotch stocks at competitive prices helps Indian distilleries to improve blending quality for premium whisky products while maintaining cost efficiency, experts said. However, the latest dispute poses a major threat to these operations.

The lack of preferential tariffs on bulk Scotch could have "substantial" financial implications for importers, Parth Shah, partner at Deloitte India, told CNBC. "This could particularly affect Indian manufacturers who have invested in domestic blending and bottling operations using imported bulk Scotch whisky," he added.

Diageo-owned United Spirits, Pernod Ricard and Indian firms Allied Blenders and Radico Khaitan own and market some of the popular whisky brands in India.

Companies and industry bodies, the International Spirits and Wine Association and Confederation of Indian Alcoholic Beverage Companies, did not respond to CNBC's requests for comment.

A spokesperson for one of the whisky makers told CNBC that industry bodies were in talks with the commerce ministry to "represent adherence to the India-UK FTA." The person did not wish to be identified as they fear it could lead to a potential backlash from regulators.

"The lesson for the EU and other partners negotiating with India is that securing a headline tariff reduction is only half the battle," Bhattacharya of Maplecroft Verisk said, adding that partners need to pay close attention to whether "concessions translate into actual market access."

#2845. Mike Brown pushing for faster offense from sluggish Knicks

"Last-last," Brown asked, "or second-to-last?"

Entering Saturday, the Knicks sat at the very bottom of the NBA in pace, averaging 94.91 possessions per game. Brown repeatedly has urged his team to play with pace since taking over last year, but it's a rare desire that has not come to fruition.

New York Knicks head coach Mike Brown reacts during the fourth quarter against the Portland Trail Blazers in New York, Saturday, Nov. 21, 2026. Getty Images

The reality is that this is not an overly athletic roster that thrives in transition. This group of veteran players never will be a unit that loves to get up and down the floor. Jalen Brunson, Karl-Anthony Towns and OG Anunoby are more methodical scorers who prefer to set up themselves in the frontcourt.

Brown knows that, so while he will continue to preach pace, he values something else that serves as an indication of whether his offense is functioning properly, something that doesn't necessarily require the natural athleticism they lack.

"We have to make sure that every time we cut, it's a scoring cut and our decisions are quick," Brown said before a 97-96 Knicks win, which required a buzzer-beating 3 from Mikal Bridges to escape after blowing a 26-point second-half lead.

When the Knicks offense kicked into gear during their championship run last year, it was evident.

With Towns operating as a hub, the four other Knicks on the floor constantly cut and set off-ball screens around him. The ball moved quickly, swinging side to side constantly. Towns, along with Anunoby and Jordan Clarkson, did not play Saturday.

Landry Shamet, who scored 20 points, shoots a jumper over Toumani Camara during the Knicks' buzzer-beating win over the Trail Blazers on Nov. 21, 2026, at the Garden. Jason Szenes for New York Post

"You can tell when a guy goes from Point A to Point B with a little bit of velocity," Brown said. "A guy like Landry Shamet, he is doing it every single time. With him shooting the ball the way he's shooting it, that gravity that he carries when he moves, you can't really see it or feel it if you don't know the game well. But when he moves that quickly from Point A to Point B, everybody is just leaning just a little bit in that direction, which may create an opening or an advantage for your teammate that's away from him."

It's a reason why Shamet's role keeps growing. Among the five starters, though, it's something they've had to reestablish after a sluggish first few games of the season.

The Knicks aren't quick in transition. Once they're set in the frontcourt, though, they're capable of playing quickly.

It's when they are at their best. It takes pressure off Brunson having to orchestrate everything. It sets up open catch-and-shoot opportunities for Anunoby and Mikal Bridges. It allows Josh Hart to utilize his passing ability and set up teammates.

"Trying to get everybody to do that all the time is huge," Brown said. "Because the defense doesn't have a chance to catch up, they don't have a chance to get set, and they just gotta react. And sometimes they'll overreact because you're going so hard. And you can see it as the game goes along. If guys aren't doing it, we try to mention it to him."

A slow team finding a way to play fast — that's crucial to the Knicks' success.

#2842. Ravens doomed by 4 turnovers vs. Panthers, including fumble through end zone in back-and-forth thriller

Justice Hill thought he had a touchdown Sunday against the Carolina Panthers.

Instead, the Baltimore Ravens running back fumbled through the end zone to spark a wild sequence of events that ultimately resulted in a score for the Panthers.

The sequence proved critical as the Panthers went on to a 35-33 win that was sealed by a go-ahead Panthers field goal followed up by a Jacoby Brissett interception.

With the win, the Panthers improved to 5-5 to increase their lead in the NFC South to two games over the rest of the division. The Bucs, Falcons and Saints are all 3-7.

With 12:17 remaining in the second quarter and Baltimore holding a 12-7 lead, Hill took a handoff from the Carolina 37-yard line and broke free through the secondary toward the end zone. But just before he reached the end zone, Carolina cornerback Will Lee punched the ball out of of Hill's grasp.

The ball then bounced through the end zone to trigger perhaps the most controversial rule in football. Even though the Panthers didn't recover the loose ball, they took over possession via a touchback.

Just like that, a would-be Ravens score turned into a turnover at the last moment thanks to a heads-up play from Lee.

The Panthers didn't initially do much with their good fortune. They punted after a 3-and-out on their ensuing possession. But another Ravens miscue set them up with another opportunity.

This time, LaJohntay Wester muffed the punt return, allowing the Panthers to recover deep in Baltimore territory.

This time, the Panthers capitalized. Two plays later, Chuba Hubbard ran 18 yards around the left edge for a touchdown and a 14-12 Carolina lead.

All was not lost for the Ravens in the wild sequence.

The very next touch for the Ravens resulted in a score. Devontez Walker returned the ensuing kickoff 86 yards for a touchdown to put the Ravens back on top with a 19-14 lead.

If you're counting, that's three consecutive Ravens touches that resulted in a fumble through the end zone, a muffed punt that led to a Carolina touchdown and a kickoff return for a Baltimore touchdown.

But Baltimore's lead would not hold. The lead changed hands five more times from there in a back-and-forth battle that gave Panthers fans in Charlotte their money's worth.

The teams exchanged two more touchdowns before halftime, and the Ravens went into the break with a 26-22 advantage. Carolina then opened the second half with a touchdown for a 29-26 lead that that the Ravens took back on a 15-yard Derrick Henry touchdown run with 6:32 remaining.

That left Carolina with the ball and a 33-32 deficit.

Bryce Young led a 12-play, 55-yard drive from there to set up Ryan Fitzgerald for a 37-yard field goal and a 35-33 Panthers lead with 1:44 remaining. From there, Tyrel Dodson iced the win with an interception of Brissett.

Brissett evaded pressure on the first-and-10 play from the Baltimore 32. But he threw an ill-advised pass off his back foot that resulted in a batted ball in the secondary that Dodson caught.

The late interception encapsulated a day in which the Panthers secured a 4-1 advantage in takeaways. Henry lost a fumble in addition to the turnovers by Brissett, Hill and Wester. The Ravens out-gained the Panthers in total offensive yardage, 421 to 378, but their miscues ultimately cost them the game.

The Ravens also lost Henry to injury. He left the field after his fourth-quarter touchdown for concussion evaluation. His status wasn't immediately clear postgame.

The loss drops a disappointing Ravens team to 4-7, well out of the AFC playoff picture.

#2840. State and federal battle over prediction markets regulation heats up after midterms

The turf war between states and the federal government is intensifying after Democrats secured control of Congress and key governorships on midterm elections night.

While it was widely expected Democrats would step up oversight of the emerging, multibillion-dollar industry if they won control of Congress, the actions after the elections have come from primarily Republican-led states.

At the same time, the Commodity Futures Trading Commission — the federal regulator for prediction markets —  has ramped up its oversight of "mention" markets, where traders speculate on what individuals will say during a speech or broadcast. It's a type of event contract the agency has said is susceptible to manipulation.

CNBC first reported on Tuesday that the CFTC placed a hold on the certification of markets related to President Donald Trump's words in mid-November. Kalshi said it was complying with the order, but CEO Tarek Mansour said at an Axios event on Tuesday that the company disagreed with the regulatory decision.

On Thursday, the Senate passed a bipartisan bill that would ban CFTC-registered entities from listing contracts involving terrorism, assassination, war or military operations. The measure also bars contracts that are "reasonably expected" to resolve based on an individual's death.

Utah Attorney General Derek Brown filed 14 criminal charges against Kalshi and its founders, Mansour and Luana Lopes Lara, on Oct. 28, asserting the company violated a state law that prohibits gambling.

Kalshi's founders are expected to appear in person at Utah's Third District Court on Dec. 4, according to court documents.

"This is a crazy overreach; Utah is clearly playing politics. We look forward to putting a stop to this in court, just like we did in Arizona," said Kalshi's spokesperson Dani Lever.

In a separate, civil complaint on Oct. 20, Utah also named institutional market maker Susquehanna International Group as a defendant, along with Kalshi.

Though Arizona Attorney General Kris Mayes filed a criminal complaint against Kalshi in March, a federal judge blocked the case from proceeding in April.

Louisiana's Republican attorney general also filed a complaint to ban multiple exchanges from operating in the state on Nov. 12. A state judge ordered a temporary restraining order against the prediction market companies that same day.

"Who would have thought that these red states, that were reluctant to jump into the litigation arena, ended up having the most boldest and most innovative solutions," said gambling attorney Daniel Wallach, noting that Louisiana and Utah's latest lawsuits were led by Republican attorneys general.

In response, the CFTC sued Louisiana on Nov. 13. Separately, the federal agency filed suit against Arkansas after that state pursued civil enforcement against Kalshi.

The CFTC has sued 12 states as it tries to defend what it views as its exclusive jurisdiction to regulate prediction markets.

Meanwhile, the CFTC may soon add commissioners. President Donald Trump sent bipartisan nominations to the U.S. Senate on Nov. 12 to fill two of the four open seats on the commission. Chairman Michael Selig has been the only member since his nomination was approved by the Senate in December 2025.

Nathan Anonick, a Republican, and Matthew Rowland, a Democrat, appeared before the Senate Agriculture Committee on Tuesday. Multiple senators questioned them on the issue of prediction markets. Both demurred when asked whether they viewed sports-related event contracts as gambling.

The CFTC's position faced another setback in a complicated decision by the Fourth U.S. Circuit Court of Appeals this week. The court ruled 2-1 that Maryland can enforce its state gambling law against Kalshi, though the judges disagreed on the reasoning.

"Judges can't agree with each other on why we're wrong," Kalshi spokesperson Elisabeth Diana said in a statement about the decision. "This splintered decision, including three separate contradictory opinions, demonstrates there is no unified coherent legal argument against prediction markets."

Overall, prediction markets have now seen three losses and one win at the circuit court level. It's widely expected the question of whether states or the federal government can regulate sports-related event contracts will be decided by the Supreme Court.

Kalshi on Nov. 12  ended its advisory relationship with Donald Trump Jr. The prediction platform pointed to his venture capital firm 1789 Capital having a stake in rival platform Polymarket.

Though 1789 Capital announced its holding in Polymarket in August, Kalshi and the president's son ended their relationship after Democrats secured a majority in the House and Senate. Democratic lawmakers are expected to probe companies with relationships to the Trump family.

Nine Democratic senators, led by Adam Schiff, D-California, on Monday sent a letter to the CFTC asking it to reverse a directive to platforms not to geofence their offerings when they receive unfavorable court rulings stopping exchanges from operating in certain states.

Geofencing involves the use of location technology to block users in certain jurisdictions from participating in events contracts.

"Your unilateral decision directly conflicts with active court orders, undermines state and tribal law, and must be reversed," the senators wrote.

Meanwhile, Democratic Rep. Ro Khanna, D-California, has called for Congress to ban sports-related prediction markets entirely.

"Kalshi, a $40B company, lets 18 year olds bet on sports. They call it a 'prediction market.' It's gambling," he wrote in a post on X on Wednesday. "Investigate and ban sports prediction markets."

Disclosure: CNBC and Kalshi have a commercial relationship that includes customer acquisition and a minority investment.

#2839. A trading strategy to ride out the Nvidia uncertainty and generate income in the process

Jensen Huang's abrupt resignation has sent shockwaves through the tech sector, dragging Nvidia's (NVDA) stock down roughly 30% since Oct. 6. The market despises uncertainty, and losing a visionary founder, particularly one without a well-telegraphed succession plan, makes it worse.

However, for opportunistic investors, this panic has made the stock cheaper (obviously) while options have become significantly more expensive. Trading in the $160s, Nvidia's valuation has taken a big haircut, yet its underlying business fundamentals remain largely intact. While Huang was the architect of Nvidia's AI dominance, the company's moat — superior GPUs, the CUDA software ecosystem, and networking expertise — does not walk out the door with him. The company's structural advantage in the global artificial intelligence rollout persists.

Buying good companies at a discount is investing 101, but the current market dynamics may do something else for those who use options.  Huang's departure guarantees that Nvidia will be operating under a cloud of transition for the foreseeable future. Executing a permanent CEO search while navigating global regulatory scrutiny takes time. Consequently, Nvidia's implied volatility (IV)—the market's pricing of expected future price swings — has spiked to highs not seen since the tariff tantrum in the first half of 2025, and this elevated IV is highly likely to persist for several months as the leadership drama slowly unfolds.

This prolonged period of high implied volatility can be a goldmine for option sellers, specifically those executing options overlays such as covered call strategies. When you buy NVDA shares at these depressed levels and simultaneously sell out-of-the-money call options against your position, the premiums you collect are directly tied to IV. Because fear and transition-related uncertainty have dramatically inflated these option prices, the income generated from selling covered calls is now significantly higher than usual.

This lowers the risk profile of holding the stock considerably over time. Consistently collecting these inflated premiums systematically lowers your overall cost basis in the underlying shares. If the stock trades sideways or recovers slowly during the CEO search, you are generating an outsized cash yield on a stagnant asset. If the stock experiences further downside turbulence, the heavy premium income acts as a substantial downside buffer, meaningfully reducing the strategy's overall risk over time.

By deploying a persistent covered call strategy, investors can leverage the exact fear driving the stock down to generate robust, risk-reducing income while maintaining exposure to the undeniable future of AI.
