#2809. The Chiefs took care of business in soft three-game stretch

It's time for the Kansas City Chiefs to turn the page to a perennially important matchup with the AFC's No. 1 seed, the Buffalo Bills, on Thanksgiving night. It's the first of three road games against teams with winning records (the Los Angeles Rams on the following Thursday before squaring off with the Cincinnati Bengals).

The anticipation for this game has built since the Chiefs' last-second loss to the Denver Broncos in Week 8, which sent Kansas City to a 5-2 record. Since then, the softest three-game stretch of the team's schedule provided a chance to ramp up before squaring off with Buffalo.

In that time, the Chiefs seldom played with their food, keeping their foot on the gas pedal against the New York Jets, Atlanta Falcons, and Arizona Cardinals for a combined 104-32 score. Backup quarterback Justin Fields took the final snaps for the offense in all three games, including almost the entirety of the fourth quarter against the Cardinals.

It's exactly what the team needed ahead of the rest of the schedule, seemingly filled with playoff contenders.

The Chief took care of business in style, too. The team has averaged 422 total yards over the last four weeks, including the loss to Denver, a rate that would lead the NFL for the season by more than 20 yards.

Quarterback Patrick Mahomes has also been dominant in this stretch, rarely losing rhythm with the Chiefs' offense since the debacle against the Seattle Seahawks. He has not thrown an interception since that game nearly a month ago.

There was only so much Kansas City could prove about its odds of winning the Super Bowl this year in the last three wins, but the team did seemingly all anyone in Chiefs Kingdom could ask for: dominate bad teams thoroughly.

Now, the Chiefs need to take the next step and find a way to win crucial road games, especially against conference foes vying for the No. 1 seed like Buffalo and Cincinnati.

#2808. ‘It worked every time': How Sixers found a clutch answer at shootaround

'It worked every time': How Sixers found a clutch answer at shootaround originally appeared on NBC Sports Philadelphia

The Sixers created their two cleanest late-game looks several hours before they needed them.

During Wednesday morning's shootaround in Orlando, Nick Nurse had the Sixers work on offensive material they had not consistently used this season. They tested one of those actions during the first half, returned to it throughout the closing minutes and watched it produce the shots they needed to complete a 17-point comeback.

"We worked on some stuff this morning that we hadn't used before, and they ran all that stuff very, very well and very calmly and with composure," Nurse told reporters.

The Sixers have earned six of their 13 wins after trailing by double digits. They are also 11-0 in games that reached clutch time.

Relying on comebacks is not a sustainable plan. The repeated late-game situations, however, have forced a new roster to respond to different lineups, coverages and responsibilities while the season is still young.

Each close finish gives Nurse more information about which players and actions hold up when possessions tighten. Wednesday gave him a new option.

Anfernee Simons' clinching three in the Sixers' 121-116 win over the Magic was the final result of that preparation.

"That last play, we ran that play in the first half," Simons said. "We worked it a couple of times, and in the second half we ran that play every single time down the stretch, and it worked every time.

"It was a play we ran through. [It's gratifying] especially when it's working consistently and you're getting different shots out of it and the opposing team just don't know what to do.

"We just continue to run the play and get the open shots we need."

NBC Sports Philadelphia video producer and Sixers Talk podcast co-host Kevin Rice broke down how the Sixers used its different options to keep Orlando guessing.

The action begins with two players stationed near the elbows. A player on the opposite elbow then sets a screen for the player up top, who receives the ball while moving around it.

The Sixers ran the set three consecutive times during the first half. None of the possessions reached the primary option. Each still produced a quality opportunity.

On the first, Joel Embiid cleared LeBron James out of the area and directed Simons around him for a dribble handoff. Simons received an open three-point look.

Orlando took away the handoff the next time down but left Embiid one-on-one against Wendell Carter Jr. Embiid created enough room to retrieve his own miss and convert an and-one opportunity.

The third attempt pulled Orlando's defense into the paint. Two passes moved the ball through the collapsing coverage and created an open three for VJ Edgecombe.

"We had a little thing where he comes flying off behind Joel, and he doesn't get to do that that much, right?" Nurse said of Simons. "So tonight he gets the opportunity to do it, and it was very good for us on a number of plays."

The Sixers changed the action late without abandoning its initial Horns alignment. Simons moved through the lane to help form a Spain pick-and-roll involving James and Embiid.

On the first late possession, Simons remained near the play after James gained a matchup against Carter. Orlando struggled to sort out its assignments, leaving James open for a 19-foot jumper with 54.8 seconds remaining.

After Jalen Suggs made one of two free throws, the Sixers returned to the same setup. This time, Simons relocated to the left corner after James drew Carter on a switch.

James drove into the paint. Suggs lost track of Simons while responding to the penetration, and James sent the ball toward the corner.

Simons buried the open three to give the Sixers a 119-113 lead with 15 seconds left.

"Bron just penetrated the paint," Simons said. "It kind of got some confusion, and when I was on the dunker and kind of spaced out to the corner, I ended up finding myself wide open for the three, and Bron found me."

The Sixers entered the night without Tyrese Maxey and Jaylen Brown, their two leading scorers. Simons made his first start with Philadelphia and recorded 18 points in the city where he grew up.

His final basket also illustrated why Nurse believes the Sixers have more to uncover with him.

"When he's out there, they gotta be really careful," Nurse said. "So I think there's some drives he creates, and just about every time he lets it go, whether he's on balance or not, you think it's going in, and that is a big deal. Like, that is a big deal.

"And again, I think we're still a ways away from really unlocking him to being even more part of some of the things we can do with him to create more shots."

Philadelphia is now 11-0 in clutch games. The finish in Orlando provided a more specific explanation than experience or shot-making alone.

The Sixers installed a set in the morning, explored its counters and trusted it when the game tightened. James supplied the patience to read Orlando's coverage. Embiid occupied the middle of the action. Simons moved without the ball, and Edgecombe remained ready to shoot.

James said his heart rate drops when close games increase the pressure on everyone else.

"I mean, experience," James said. "A guy that's seen every situation this league has to offer. It's nothing I haven't seen. And, you know, in the moments where people's heart rate goes up or anxiety goes up, mine is pretty low. It's actually lower, you know."

The Sixers still have more actions and reads to learn as Nurse becomes familiar with his new personnel, but their latest win showed their innate ability to adapt in big moments.

"I think we're still a long ways away from knowing all the reads and all that stuff because it's just a little early, right?" Nurse said.

#2807. Saudi Arabia, Bahrain report new attacks as Iran says it remains open to a deal with U.S.

Saudi Arabia and Bahrain reported attacks by Iran-linked forces over the weekend, as Iran's foreign minister said his government remains open to a deal to end the war with the U.S.

"Intercepted and destroyed a drone launched by the Houthi terrorist militia toward the Riyadh area," the Saudi-led Coalition to Support Legitimacy in Yemen, which has been leading the fight against the Iran-backed group, said Sunday in a post on X.

The coalition said late Saturday that its air defenses "intercepted and destroyed a ballistic missile" launched by the Yemen-based Houthis toward Saudi Arabia's western region, which includes some of the country's sensitive energy refining and export infrastructure.

Neighboring Bahrain also said it came under Iranian fire.

"As a result of the heinous Iranian aggression, a civilian industrial facility was targeted, causing a fire at the site," Bahrain's Ministry of Interior said on X. The ministry later said two people, a Bahraini citizen and a Pakistani resident, were injured in the attack.

Bahrain is home to the U.S. Navy's Fifth Fleet.

Iranian state media reported late Saturday that the country's Islamic Revolutionary Guard Corps fired missiles and drones at U.S. military facilities in Bahrain.

A separate Iranian media report claimed the IRGC's air defenses intercepted and destroyed a U.S. MQ-9 drone.

U.S. Central Command did not immediately respond to CNBC's request for comment on either Iranian report.

Meanwhile, Iranian Foreign Minister Abbas Araghchi said his government remains open to a negotiated settlement to the war. An agreement aimed at developing a permanent peace deal signed in June between President Donald Trump and his Iranian counterpart Masoud Pezeshkian — the so-called Islamabad Memorandum of Understanding — later broke down.

"The Islamabad Memorandum of Understanding is no longer on the table and has no implementation relevance," Araghchi told the Iran newspaper.

"However, the commitments that the U.S. had accepted in that Memorandum of Understanding and the principle that the implementation of those commitments can pave the way for reaching a final agreement are still accepted by both sides," he said.

The war, which began with U.S. and Israeli airstrikes on Iranian targets on Feb. 28, has resulted in major disruptions to energy shipments through the Strait of Hormuz between Iran and Oman.

The United Kingdom Maritime Trade Operations reported that two tankers near Oman were struck by unidentified projectiles on Saturday.

Iran has imposed curbs on vessels moving through the strait, a major sticking point in negotiations with the U.S.

Oil prices have fallen for three straight weeks, however, as traders weighed strong Middle East flows and rising U.S. inventories.

International benchmark Brent crude fell 3.4% for the week, while U.S. crude tumbled 5.2%.

#2806. Tarik Skubal return to Detroit as a Met: When will it happen?

Are Detroit Tigers fans ready to see Tarik Skubal in another team's uniform in the Motor City (again)?

With the left-hander agreeing to a record $450 million free-agent deal (over nine seasons) with the New York Mets, we can start counting the days till the ex-Tiger's second return to Detroit as an opponent.

Skubal made a start at Comerica Park last season after he was dealt from the Tigers to the Los Angeles Dodgers at the trade deadline. On Aug. 28, Skubal allowed one run while striking out seven with no walks over six innings in a 2-1 Dodgers win.

MEANWHILE, IN DETROIT: Tigers sign RHP Luis Gil to $2.3M deal

The crowd at Comerica Park had mixed reactions, with a pregame ovation followed by mid-game boos.

Of course, that was back when Skubal was merely the two-time reigning AL Cy Young winner and fans had hopes for a reunion in free agency. (It was also before we learned Skubal reclaimed the espresso machine he gifted the Tigers clubhouse.)

So when will fans at Fifth Third Park – remember, the Tigers' home has a new moniker for 2027 – get to let Skubal know how they feel about his new deal, which makes him the highest paid pitcher in MLB history?

A quick glance at the Mets' schedule shows them set to visit Detroit early in the season, with a three-game series set for Fifth Third Park on April 9-11.

And assuming Skubal is the Mets' Opening Day starter, and that they stick with a five-man rotation, and that they don't skip any starts … Skubal looks likely to get the start in the series finale, slated for 1:40 p.m., Sunday, April 11. (He could even face two of the players acquired in the deal that sent him to L.A., as right-hander River Ryan is a candidate for the Opening Day rotation and outfielder Zyhir Hope will have a chance to claim a roster spot in spring training.)

Of course, all this is assuming that the impending lockout by MLB owners – made even more impending now that the Mets have stepped on MLB's salary scale even further – is all cleared up with a new collective bargaining agreement in time to start the season on time, on March 26.

That may be the biggest assumption of all.

#2805. Nvidia has lost $2 trillion in value. Here's what options traders expect from earnings

Jensen Huang's forced exit from Nvidia shocked Wall Street and Washington, wiped $2 trillion off its market value, and raised serious questions about national security and regulation of the AI complex. Traders, for the moment, must content themselves with the comparatively mundane business of Tuesday's earnings, and options traders are expecting big moves.

With volatility in the stock near the highest since April 2025, options traders are now pricing a 7% reaction in the stock after its report, the biggest implied move going into an Nvidia report since February 2025, and well above the median move of 3.2% over the last eight quarters.

Implied volatility reached 68 Monday, compared to under 40 in early October, according to ThinkOrSwim data. Nvidia vol reached a multi-year high above 92 in the aftermath of President Trump's tariff Liberation day in April '25. In other words, the company that for much of the AI boom was the most reliable winner in the market, is now more volatile than all but 35 companies in the S&P 500.

"Longer term, I see significant downside risk unless Nvidia can attract an exceptional leader, someone like Tim Cook or AMD's Lisa Su," Nigam Arora, founder of the Arora report, said in a text ahead of the company's after-market report on Tuesday.

Since hitting all-time highs in October, Nvidia shares have fallen 35%, shedding $2 trillion in market capitalization along the way. Including Nvidia, there are only five companies in the S&P 500 worth more than $2 trillion. However, there is evidence that bulls have been holding out hope while bears snatch up profits: the ratio of open puts to call contracts has actually widened towards calls, according to Barchart data. The ratio is currently 0.87, down from a one-year high of 1.02 two weeks ago and the lowest since the first week of October.

The most popular contract by volume on Monday was the 155-strike put expiring at the bell Monday, but the most popular trade for later expiry was the 160-strike call expiring Thursday, and the second-most-common purchase was the 190-strike call expiring Friday, SpotGamma data show.

One catch is that most call contracts traded Monday looked like neutral to bearish positions opened by traders selling calls. Of the 5 million options traded on Monday, 3 million were calls, but more were likely opened by sellers than buyers. Of the $2.3 billion in options premium traded on Nvidia Monday, $1.2 billion was tied to puts, SpotGamma data show.

With bears taking profits in puts the past month, there's another potential snag on open interest. There are very few open put contracts at or below the $157 level at which the stock closed Monday. That suggests any bad surprise would find many traders with little downside protection.

By the same token, there's notable open interest in call contracts from the 175-strike and above, suggesting profit-taking on a bounce could limit Nvidia's recovery past levels it breached over the past week.

Either way, Tuesday's earnings could provide fireworks.

#2804. Cowboys vs. Titans: 5 winners and 3 losers from the game

The Dallas Cowboys entered Sunday knowing there was very little margin left for error in their season.

Against the Tennessee Titans, the offense responded in emphatic fashion. Dallas moved the football almost at will and built a commanding advantage, although some familiar discipline and defensive problems prevented the performance from being completely clean.

Either way, the Cowboys get the home victory winning 51-30. Here are today's winners and losers from the win at home.

Dak Prescott completely controlled this game. Prescott went 28-of-33 for 302 yards and four touchdown passes, while also adding a rushing touchdown himself. He also connected on a franchise-record 19 consecutive passes. He has found answers at every level of the field and, most importantly, hasn't given Tennessee anything through turnovers.

The efficiency was absolutely outstanding. Prescott connected with CeeDee Lamb, George Pickens and Javonte Williams for touchdowns and repeatedly punished Tennessee when it tried to bring pressure or crowd the line. A testament to his control in this game, Prescott hit eight different receivers in this game and rushed three times for 10 yards.

Javonte Williams hasn't produced a massive rushing total, but he keeps finding the end zone. Williams scored Dallas' opening touchdown from one yard before later catching an 11-yard touchdown pass from Prescott in the third quarter.

That gives Dallas another example of why Williams has become such an important part of this offense. He's capable of doing the dirty work around the goal line while also contributing as a receiver when defenses focus too heavily on Lamb and Pickens. Plus he still provides quality pass protection.

This was a great example of just how difficult the Cowboys receiver room can be to defend when everybody contributes.

It starts with CeeDee Lamb, who produced another monster afternoon with 11 catches for 132 yards and a touchdown. Tennessee knew where the football was going and still couldn't stop him. Lamb consistently moved the chains, produced yards after the catch and scored from eight yards out in the second quarter.

Then there was George Pickens, who gave Dallas the complementary threat it needs opposite Lamb. Pickens reached seven receptions for 102 yards and a touchdown, averaging 14.6 yards per catch. His 10-yard touchdown in the second quarter helped Dallas build a 27-6 advantage. He also added a six-yard rushing attempt, showing how Dallas continues finding different ways to get the football into his hands.

And then there's KaVontae Turpin. His contribution didn't come through the traditional passing game, but he's still part of what makes this receiver room so difficult to defend. Dallas gave Turpin two designed rushing attempts and he turned them into 41 yards and a touchdown, including a 22-yard score around the left side.

James Houston made the most of his opportunities. His biggest play came late in the first quarter when he exploded off the edge and dropped Cam Ward for an eight-yard sack on third down, immediately ending Tennessee's possession. Houston had already created pressure on the previous snap and consistently looked disruptive when Dallas allowed him to attack.

He actually got to Ward again during the second quarter for what would have been a 13-yard sack, only for a Joey Porter Jr. facemask penalty to wipe the play away. The sack didn't count statistically, but the rep still showed Houston beating his blocker and finishing at the quarterback. He finally got that sack back later in the game.

Houston keeps making the argument that he deserves more opportunities to provide it, and a day with two sacks that could have been three is a good starting point for that argument.

Quinnen Williams continues to look like the centerpiece Dallas hoped it was acquiring. Williams recorded a sack of Cam Ward in the second quarter, getting through the interior to drop him for a loss. He also produced one of his best run-defense snaps of the afternoon by exploding into the backfield and dropping former Cowboy Tony Pollard for a two-yard loss.

Williams also affected the passing game without always appearing in the sack column, including forcing an incompletion on third down later in the second quarter. He also tipped to balls at the line of scrimmage.

The high point of what he did today, however, was the great strip-sack late in the game. Other than an interception, Williams marked off every major defensive statistical category today, and no one does that by accident.

Mike Pettine's first game calling the defense has produced some encouraging moments. The explosive-play problem hasn't disappeared.

The clearest example came in the third quarter when rookie Carnell Tate got loose for a 59-yard touchdown, immediately answering a Dallas score and allowing Tennessee to remain within striking distance.

The Cowboys can produce several excellent defensive snaps, generate pressure and force an opponent behind schedule, only for a lapse in concentration to wipe all of it away. That has been the story of this defense throughout the season.

This is the part of Dallas' performance that Brian Schottenheimer will hate when he watches the film. The Cowboys committed 11 penalties for 128 yards, that's far too much free yardage against anyone, never mind for a team that has spent most of the season fighting its own discipline problems.

George Pickens' third-quarter taunting penalty was particularly unnecessary. Prescott had just scored to extend Dallas' advantage, yet Pickens immediately gave away 15 yards after the play.

Dallas has been good enough offensively to overcome the penalties today, but a different team on a different day this is bad.

The Cowboys defense wasn't suddenly dominant against Tennessee, but it looked different this week, and that isn't a particularly good look for Christian Parker.

Tennessee still created explosive plays and scored points, but their touchdowns during the meat of the game came from a long pass interference penalty, and the failure to touch Tate down on what should have been a medium gain.

What stood out, though, was how much more unified the unit appeared. Communication looked cleaner, players seemed more certain of their assignments and there appeared to be less confusion before the snap.

Dallas also generated pressure at important moments and generally looked more organized despite Pettine only recently joining the staff. If the defense immediately looks calmer and more connected in the very first game after somebody else takes over the play-calling, questions are naturally going to be asked about what was happening before.

Pettine hasn't fixed the Cowboys defense in one afternoon, but even a noticeable step toward looking like a coherent unit is a huge jump from what was happening.

#2803. Anthropic’s record IPO collides with tech’s worst week on Wall Street since 2020

Startup investor Ben Narasin is so jazzed about Anthropic's blockbuster IPO that he's planning a party later this month at his Silicon Valley home. Sake, vodka and Krug Champagne will be served. Each attendee will be given two jars of caviar.

"This is a celebration of the most material, significant, and mind-blowingly awesome event that has ever happened in venture capital," Narasin, founding partner of Tenacity Venture Capital, told CNBC in an interview, adding that he didn't even invest in the company.

Anthropic raised a record $93.5 billion in its IPO and an additional $26 billion from tech giants that bought at the offer price. The stock gained 17% in its debut on Thursday, lifting Anthropic's market cap to $1.8 trillion and providing a much-needed windfall for Silicon Valley's startup ecosystem.

It was quite the split-screen moment on Wall Street, which spent the week in an AI-driven tailspin. Led by Nvidia's 23% plunge, which marked the stock's sharpest weekly pullback in 18 years, the Nasdaq slid 10% over the past five trading days in its worst week since the early days of Covid in 2020.

Investors and analysts spent the week debating whether Anthropic will trade alongside AI hardware, as it's reliant on advanced chips, memory and data centers, or whether it will stand alone as the first public pure-play AI developer and a company that's achieved unrivaled growth in less than six years of existence.

That question will likely persist for some time, but Friday's market action showed that Wall Street is skeptical of Anthropic's ability to grow into its lofty valuation. At week's end, the Claude creator's $1.6 trillion fully diluted market cap put it behind just six U.S. companies: Apple, Microsoft, Alphabet, Nvidia, Amazon and SpaceX.

With the tech market in turmoil due to rising interest rates, raging anti-AI sentiment and Nvidia's C-suite crisis, Anthropic's IPO was part exuberance, part relief for an industry hungry for exits.

After venture-backed IPOs yielded $73 billion in proceeds in 2021, the amount raised in the next four years totaled just $39 billion, according to data from University of Florida emeritus professor Jay Ritter. In 2021, 126 technology companies went public, compared with 34 in 2025.

Meanwhile, the private market has continued to balloon, with companies raising ever-larger sums at sky-high valuations without the pressures of quarterly earnings. Many startups that had hoped to go public early in the decade fell out of favor in the AI boom. They experienced hefty job cuts, down rounds and acquisitions for pennies on the dollar.

"There was a time in Silicon Valley not that long ago where the prevailing wisdom was the default path should be avoiding going public for as long as possible," said Somesh Dash, a general partner at venture firm IVP. The view was, "it's too long, it's too hard, it's not worth it," he said.

Anthropic is very young compared to some of those companies, but it was a huge collector of private capital. Investors poured $141 billion into the AI lab as of September, according to Anthropic's prospectus.

For a few Silicon Valley firms that contributed to those funding rounds, the returns are unlike anything they've ever seen. At the IPO price, Menlo Ventures and Lightspeed Venture Partners each owned about $25 billion worth of Anthropic stock. Bessemer Venture Partners owned a stake worth roughly $10 billion, partner Byron Deeter said.

Matt Murphy, a partner at Menlo, told CNBC's David Faber on Thursday that his firm has more than five limited partners "that we delivered over $1 billion of returns to."

"At the end of the day, that's really our job, is to get dollars back into foundations and endowments," Murphy said. "There's a lot of pride in being able to do that."

Bessemer's Deeter was also jubilant on Thursday, posting on X that, in addition to being "the largest bet in Bessemer's history," the IPO "is a punctuation mark in the history of both humanity and computation."

That's quite a change from a few years earlier, when Anthropic was hardly generating revenue, and success was far from guaranteed. In 2023, Anthropic's Claude 2 was struggling to keep pace with models from Google and OpenAI. In April 2024, investors at Bessemer wrote in a memo that if Anthropic missed its own targets, there was "a good chance that we lose all or part of our money."

Guru Chahal at Lightspeed told CNBC that Anthropic was clearly the team taking a more measured approach to AI. The company looked beyond Nvidia's dominant AI chips, choosing to use alternatives from Amazon and Google as well. Anthropic said it generated $1.72 in revenue per dollar spent on computing power in the third quarter, up from 68 cents a year earlier.

"It is true that it required lots of capital, but when you dig in and you realize that capital is going toward compute, and this is the team that's positioned to use that compute in the most effective way, then we could connect those dots," Chahal said. He added that those metrics showed it was "going to be a very high-quality business."

Anthropic isn't the only big win for VCs this year.

Five months before Anthropic's Nasdaq debut, SpaceX hit the market in what, until this week, was by far the largest IPO in history. SpaceX, which Elon Musk founded in 2002, produced outsized returns for firms including Sequoia and Andreessen Horowitz.

SpaceX and Anthropic alone will make 2026 a monster year for the venture industry, easily exceeding any other year in IPO proceeds. And investors in OpenAI hope appetite remains strong for trillion-dollar companies next year, as the ChatGPT maker is looking to 2027 for a potential offering.

IVP's Dash said that in his AI portfolio, "a lot of companies are starting IPO prep" even if it's only "year four, year five, year six since inception." He hopes to see at least half a dozen companies go public at valuations north of $10 billion each in the second half of 2027.

Jensen Huang's sudden departure from Nvidia has left the chip giant without a clear leader at a time when the company already faced heightened concerns about a slowdown in spending. Nvidia is a key investor in Anthropic and OpenAI and a critical supplier and financing partner for the entire AI industry, which is getting hammered across the board.

In January, Democrats will take control of the House and Senate after this month's midterm elections, which included the passage of numerous ballot measures that could stymie data center development. Anthropic investor Brad Gerstner predicted after the election that market dynamics would push Anthropic to price toward the lower end of its range.

Narasin said he wasn't even sure the company would get that far.

"I was concerned they'd delay," Narasin said.

That makes the outcome that much more impressive, he said, after Anthropic priced above its expected range and upsized the offering.

"This will be bedtime stories for years for future VCs," Narasin said. "There was a company that was created, backed by angels and later venture" that in a few short years "became the single biggest IPO of all history."

— CNBC's Ari Levy contributed to this report.

#2802. Observations after Sixers survive Bucks' late surge, reach 14-3

Observations after Sixers survive Bucks' late surge, reach 14-3 originally appeared on NBC Sports Philadelphia

The Sixers survived the Bucks' late surge and reached 14-3 on the season Saturday afternoon with a 132-129 win at Xfinity Mobile Arena.

Tyler Herro nearly sent the game to overtime with a buzzer-beating three-pointer, but replays showed that he released his shot late.

Herro had a massive performance for the 1-16 Bucks, posting a career-high 50 points on 15-for-23 shooting, 11 rebounds and seven assists.

Joel Embiid had 26 points, a season-high 17 rebounds and nine assists for the Sixers.

LeBron James (21 points), Tyrese Maxey (17 points) and Anfernee Simons (18) were among the seven Sixers to score in double figures.

The Sixers were down Jaylen Brown (left hamstring strain), Dean Wade (left hamstring tightness/soreness), Adem Bona (cervical spine sprain) and Ariel Hukporti (right Achilles tendon tear).

Next up for the Sixers is a Monday night meeting with the Trail Blazers. Here are observations on their victory over the Bucks:

Maxey returned to the Sixers' starting five after missing the team's comeback win Wednesday over the Magic with right foot soreness. He was joined by VJ Edgecombe, Kentavious Caldwell-Pope, James and Embiid.

James threw down a dunk on the day's first play. Embiid then dished to a cutting Edgecombe for a layup that put the Sixers up 4-0.

The Bucks quickly found a foothold in the game.

Embiid missed his first four shots, including two that were swatted by Myles Turner. Maxey began 1 for 5 from the floor. Herro canned a three-pointer to give Milwaukee a 15-8 lead.

The Sixers entered the game with a minus-13.2 net rating in first quarters, which ranked 28th in the NBA. They faced a 30-29 deficit after Saturday's first period.

Even with Maxey available again, Labaron Philon Jr. deservingly stayed in the Sixers' rotation following an excellent outing in Orlando.

He scored two points in six first-half minutes. The 20-year-old made a rookie mistake at the tail end of the first quarter, fouling Herro on a three-point attempt.

Dominick Barlow checked in for his first action of the season with 2:51 remaining in the first quarter. He'd been sidelined for the Sixers' first 16 games because of a lower back stress reaction.

Barlow scored his first points early in the second quarter when he tipped in a Maxey miss. He then teamed up with James in crowd-pleasing style, sprinting ahead of the pack on a fast break and flushing home the 22-time All-Star's long-distance lob.

The afternoon was not devoid of rust for Barlow. Shortly after that alley-oop, he grabbed an offensive rebound, dribbled out to the perimeter and fired an open three-pointer … which he air balled short.

To Barlow's credit, he took another three early in the fourth quarter and drained it. He logged 11 minutes and was quite productive, tallying 12 points on 5-for-6 shooting and four rebounds.

"It felt great," Barlow said of his three. "Got to shoot the ball with confidence. … I've obviously got to get a game feel; I've been missing that. But I worked too hard (in the offseason) to not shoot the ball when I'm open."

Kyle Kuzma hit a three late in the second quarter to build the Bucks' lead to 59-52. After Sixers head coach Nick Nurse's timeout, the team closed the first half on a 8-0 run featuring an Embiid dunk.

The Sixers went just 3 for 16 from long range in the first half, but their low-turnover ways helped mitigate those shooting struggles.

They committed a single turnover in the first half and finished with four giveaways. That's tied for their lowest ever on record in a single game. The Sixers have the NBA's lowest turnover percentage outside of garbage time, according to Cleaning the Glass.

"No matter who you're playing against, teams try to turn you over, they try to speed you up," James said. "Us taking care of the ball, that's a lot of the reason why we won this game."

The Sixers' shooters were bound to start sinking some open looks eventually. Simons, Caldwell-Pope and Edgecombe all made threes late in the third quarter and the team's lead grew as high as 13 points.

Thanks largely to Herro's individuals exploits, the Bucks stayed competitive. AJ Green buried a corner three to cut Milwaukee's deficit to 106-103 with 8:10 to go in the game.

James sought out his shot much more following the Sixers' timeout and did so with great success. He scored four buckets inside over the next four minutes, including a big slam that put the Sixers up 121-112.

It appeared the main questions left were whether Embiid could reach a triple-double and just how many points Herro would rack up.

However, the Bucks forced the Sixers to work down the stretch. Herro got to the 50-point milestone and trimmed the Sixers' lead to two points when he converted a layup with 19.2 seconds left.

The Sixers safely navigated to the final buzzer by the slimmest of margins. Embiid made four late free throws and everyone on the Sixers' sideline was relieved to see that Herro hadn't quite released his jumper in time.

"I try not to complain too much about any win in this league," Nurse said. "The message is we've got to finish a little better."

#2801. Tarik Skubal return to Detroit as a Met: When will it happen?

Are Detroit Tigers fans ready to see Tarik Skubal in another team's uniform in the Motor City (again)?

With the left-hander agreeing to a record $450 million free-agent deal (over nine seasons) with the New York Mets, we can start counting the days till the ex-Tiger's second return to Detroit as an opponent.

Skubal made a start at Comerica Park last season after he was dealt from the Tigers to the Los Angeles Dodgers at the trade deadline. On Aug. 28, Skubal allowed one run while striking out seven with no walks over six innings in a 2-1 Dodgers win.

MEANWHILE, IN DETROIT: Tigers sign RHP Luis Gil to $2.3M deal

The crowd at Comerica Park had mixed reactions, with a pregame ovation followed by mid-game boos.

Of course, that was back when Skubal was merely the two-time reigning AL Cy Young winner and fans had hopes for a reunion in free agency. (It was also before we learned Skubal reclaimed the espresso machine he gifted the Tigers clubhouse.)

So when will fans at Fifth Third Park – remember, the Tigers' home has a new moniker for 2027 – get to let Skubal know how they feel about his new deal, which makes him the highest paid pitcher in MLB history?

A quick glance at the Mets' schedule shows them set to visit Detroit early in the season, with a three-game series set for Fifth Third Park on April 9-11.

And assuming Skubal is the Mets' Opening Day starter, and that they stick with a five-man rotation, and that they don't skip any starts … Skubal looks likely to get the start in the series finale, slated for 1:40 p.m., Sunday, April 11. (He could even face two of the players acquired in the deal that sent him to L.A., as right-hander River Ryan is a candidate for the Opening Day rotation and outfielder Zyhir Hope will have a chance to claim a roster spot in spring training.)

Of course, all this is assuming that the impending lockout by MLB owners – made even more impending now that the Mets have stepped on MLB's salary scale even further – is all cleared up with a new collective bargaining agreement in time to start the season on time, on March 26.

That may be the biggest assumption of all.

#2799. AMD dives on short seller report of alleged China exposure

Shares of chipmaker Advanced Micro Devices close down 7.72% to $489.19 on Thursday after investment firm Hunterbrook Media issued a report alleging that the company "has its own China problem."

Hunterbrook disclosed that it had taken a short position in AMD.

The report claims that "after the U.S. blacklisted Chinese server maker IEIT Systems, the company kept getting newer AMD firmware, which enables AMD chips to run in servers."

CNBC did not independently verify this information.

Hunterbrook also said that new export rules in Washington could "tighten restrictions on AMD's foreign business," alleging that AMD "bills over 20% of its revenue to customers in China and Hong Kong," and "depends on a Chinese partner to finish most of its chips."

The allegations come amid investigations of rival chipmaker Nvidia over its sales to companies in Asia with ties to China. AMD has been viewed as a beneficiary of the uncertainty surrounding Nvidia.

"The claims misrepresent AMD's business practices and relationships," an AMD spokesperson said in a statement. "AMD complies with applicable US export control laws and regulations and maintains rigorous processes to ensure compliance across our global operations. We remain focused on serving our customers and upholding the highest standards of integrity."

Lisa Su, CEO of AMD, told investors on the company's recent earnings call that China is an "important" market and that the semiconductor giant wants to be fully compliant with U.S. export controls.

AMD continued to slide after hours, trading down 0.45% to $487 shortly after 4 p.m.

#2798. Director of National Intelligence Jay Clayton warns Americans of 'bumps in the road' after tech outages

Director of National Intelligence Jay Clayton warned Americans to prepare for "bumps in the road" after a set of damaging technology outages rocked air travel and systems like Microsoft Outlook.

Government officials have largely dismissed the prospects that the outages, which also slowed bank transactions, are linked to a coordinated or artificial intelligence cyberattack.

Clayton, however, said on CNN's "State of the Union" that outages may continue as companies and the government upgrade their systems to adapt to the AI age.

"The American people should be prepared for a great deal of investment in upgrading our systems; they should be prepared for investment in cybersecurity, and they should be prepared that any time there is a step change in technology, a step change in both compute and applications, there will be bumps in the road," Clayton said.

"You prepare by knowing that systems are going to be upgraded; they may be interrupted for a period of time and that companies and government are dealing with this on the front foot," he said.

Clayton's warning came as the U.S. prepares to celebrate Thanksgiving on Thursday, one of the busiest travel weeks of the year. A technology outage prompted a nationwide ground stop just over a week ago, which lasted for hours.

The Federal Aviation Administration said router upgrades caused a flood of data that led to the ground stop, and that there is no indication of a cyberattack.

Separately, Microsoft said the Outlook outage was caused by "an issue during an earlier network capacity upgrade."

Treasury Secretary Scott Bessent delivered a similar message on CBS' "Face the Nation."

Asked if Americans should be concerned about the security of their financial institutions, Bessent said: "No, they shouldn't."

"The patches may cause a glitch," he said, referring to measures banks are taking to safeguard from AI. "I think the American people should think of it as a vaccine; your arm may hurt, you may feel a little bad the next day, but it increases your long-term immunity and resiliency."

#2797. James sees a lot to like — and improve — for East-leading Sixers

James sees a lot to like — and improve — for East-leading Sixers originally appeared on NBC Sports Philadelphia

LeBron James likes to refer to the NBA season as a "marathon."

A few miles in, the Sixers lead the Eastern Conference pack.

With 17 regular-season contests down of 82, the team sits at 14-3, which is 2.5 games ahead of the second-seeded Celtics. The Sixers squeaked out their latest win, a 132-129 decision Saturday over the cellar-dwelling Bucks, but the bottom line is they've become accustomed to finding many routes to victory.

"We want to build habits," James said. "We want to try to take steps forward every game that we get the opportunity. I know sometimes it doesn't happen that way, but we want to do it.

"We want to strive to be as great as we can offensively, defensively and on special teams. A win is a win, but there's ways to win and ways to try to continue to build habits over the course of a marathon."

All sorts of Sixers games have gone down to the wire and they've won every single one.

The team is an incredible 12-0 in clutch situations, defined as games with a point differential of five or under in the final five minutes. The Sixers rank third in the NBA in clutch offensive rating (140.3) and third in clutch net rating (plus-42.0).

"It's good to build that part of our game," James said. "Obviously, we wanted to close the game out a lot better. But a team like that, we know they're going to be scrappy. They're going to play as hard as they can for 48 minutes. … We can learn from that."

James was the centerpiece of the Sixers' fourth-quarter offense Saturday. He scored 13 of his 21 points between the 7:46 and 3:13 mark of the fourth.

The four-time MVP got two buckets on no-nonsense drives and another on an after-timeout isolation hoop over rookie Brayden Burries. James also scored three times on a designed Horns action where he brought the ball up, used Tyrese Maxey's back screen at the elbow and caused serious problems for Milwaukee in the paint.

"We continue to add things to the half-court offense and also to the late-game package," Sixers head coach Nick Nurse said. "That's something we added a few game ago. It's one of those things where, especially if we're in the bonus, we've got some big bodies that can cut down the lane and have feel for timing. It was working. Just keep going to it."

James has grown in comfort with Nurse's coaching style. He knows the Sixers expect him to be a de facto coach on the floor, too.

"I'm just trying to be there for the team in any capacity they want," he said. "Just trying to be an extension of his calls and his commands out there on the floor. Trying to make his life and his job a little bit easier as well.

"But it's been great, not only with him but the whole coaching staff. They've welcomed me with open arms and allowed me to kind of be the voice for the team every day, and take that responsibility."

After a tremendous first impression defensively and some subpar offensive nights, the Sixers' numbers have recently evened out.

The team is 12th in offensive rating outside of garbage time and 12th in defensive rating, per Cleaning the Glass. In essence, the Sixers have been a bit above average on both ends of the floor and downright brilliant in the closing minutes.

It's all added up to an early lead in a very long race.

"I think we've bought into the pass," James said. "We have so many guys that can score the ball at elite levels. … In order for us to be a super-complete team, I think the power of the pass is very infectious. And it's also key for our other guys, to make sure they feel in a rhythm and they feel confident. We've done that from Day 1 and I think it's gotten better and better. … And I think our defense has been really good for the majority of the season.

"(Today) it wasn't so great, but we've been very good defensively throughout the course of the season. … I don't want to say there's anything bad. There's so many things for us to do better for sure. There's games where we can defensive rebound a lot better and there's games where we can not turn the ball over (as much). And there's games where we can be a little more phsyical defensively at the point of attack like (today).

"I wouldn't say anything has been bad, though. That's a good thing. We want to continue to have room for improvement and we don't want to be great in November. That would be weird."

#2796. Four Takeaways From Dodgers Losing Tarik Skubal to Mets in $450 Million Deal

The Los Angeles Dodgers will not be keeping Tarik Skubal after all, as the New York Mets get their man in free agency.

Mets owner Steven Cohen is giving Skubal $450 million over the next nine years, along with a no-trade clause and a $45 million signing bonus, according to reports.

The Dodgers, while not officially confirmed, likely fell short of that massive offer, which sets a new record for the most money ever for a starting pitcher per season and in total.

Oct 3, 2026; Los Angeles, California, USA; Los Angeles Dodgers pitcher Tarik Skubal (29) warms up before game one of the NLDS against the Atlanta Braves for the 2026 MLB Playoffs at Uniqlo Field at Dodger Stadium. Mandatory Credit: Kiyoshi Mio-Imagn Images Credit: IMAGN IMAGES via Reuters Connect (Reuters)

Here are some thoughts on what is a blow for the Dodgers, no matter how the pizza is sliced.

The Mets were in the race for Yoshinobu Yamamoto before he went to the Dodgers; the same for Kyle Tucker and even their own free agent, Edwin Diaz.

New York, after missing out on Tucker, went big after Bo Bichette, a deal that definitely didn't work out or prove to be the game-changer the team needed.

This time, with Skubal, Cohen was resolute about not coming in second place. He gave Skubal the ball, going far beyond what the records were for pitchers, to help bolster their biggest area of need.

The Dodgers still have a ton of quality pitchers in the bullpen who can be starters, not to mention Tyler Glasnow, Blake Snell, Yoshinobu Yamamoto, and Shohei Ohtani, along with an emerging Roki Sasaki.

The Mets, meanwhile, really have nothing behind Nolan McLean that comes close to the Dodgers. New York's need was urgent; beyond avoiding a loss to the Dodgers in free agency, they needed an ace who could mentor McLean and give them a true one-two punch to build around.

Sep 19, 2026; Los Angeles, California, USA; Los Angeles Dodgers pitcher Tarik Skubal (29) reacts after giving up a walk to the San Francisco Giants in the during the second inning at Dodger Stadium. Mandatory Credit: Jayne Kamin-Oncea-Imagn Images

The Mets have not been good over the last two years despite Cohen's investment, and when you look at the Dodgers' and Mets' contender status, it is no contest who gives him the best shot at winning.

However, Skubal is a pitcher who just had elbow surgery, along with Tommy John in the past, and knows the risk of pitcher injury.

To have $450 million over nine years guaranteed in a big market like New York, with the chance to help usher in a new era with the Mets, was a better pitch than possibly getting less money for the Dodgers, even if the odds of winning a title are higher.

The opportunity with the Mets is good enough to warrant the additional money, along with the No-Trade clause, massive signing bonus, etc., but made the decision to leave the Dodgers clear in the end.

Now that Skubal has officially pitched in just 12 games for the Dodgers after they gave up two legitimate top-100 prospects, it is easy to think the Dodgers made a mistake in trading for the ace.

But the reality is that the deal was worth it — Shohei Ohtani did not pitch after the trade deadline, while Tyler Glasnow was not himself after coming back from injury.

Skubal was elite for the Dodgers, posting a 2.61 ERA in the regular season, along with a 1.05 WHIP and a 2.35 FIP.

It wasn't Apex Skubal, but in the end, he delivered as expected, and with a three-peat on the line, the Dodgers needed to do everything possible to win the World Series.

They were swept in the NLCS, but LA made the right call.

#2793. Mixed day for Smyth as Saints lose in Chicago

Charlie Smyth kicked two field goals from over 50 yards but they weren't enough for the New Orleans Saints who fell to a 21-13 defeat by the Chicago Bears at Soldier Field.

It wasn't a perfect day with the boot for the 25-year-old from Mayobridge in County Down as he missed from 47 yards and sent two kickoffs out of bounds which allowed the Bears to start drives on their own 40.

Smyth signed a new contract with the NFC South franchise after twice being elevated from the practice squad in previous weeks, following Dan Carlson's release at the start of November due to mixed form.

On Sunday, the former Gaelic footballer was successful with an extra-point attempt after after quarterback Tyler Shough connected with Alvin Kamara for a touchdown.

An opportunity for a field goal in the second quarter drifted wide to the right from 47 yards, but he closed out the first half with a successful 56-yarder - one yard short of his career record - that was helped over by the crossbar.

Trailing by 11 late in the fourth quarter, the Saints drove into field goal territory and Smyth converted from 51 yards. It left them needing to recover an onside kick to get the ball back with 1.10 remaining, but his effort was easily gathered by the Bears.

The result means the Saints' record drops to 3-7, two games behind the 5-5 Carolina Panthers who lead the division.

#2792. Liam Lawson baffled by FIA Safety Car call in nightmare Las Vegas GP

Liam Lawson has questioned why it took so long for him to unlap himself at the Las Vegas Grand Prix, having already started a lap down at the Strip Circuit due to a fuel leak prior to the race.

Continuing his impressive form in 2026, Lawson was set to line up in P7 for the start of the Las Vegas GP, but the race had already begun by the time he emerged from the pit lane following a remarkable recovery job from his Racing Bulls team.

After a Lap 19 Safety Car was called after a collision between Rafa Camara and Sergio Perez, Lawson - who showed impressive pace throughout the Grand Prix - ought to have thought he had a chance of another strong race, with lapped drivers usually allowed to unlap themselves.

However, at the second longest track on the F1 calendar, Lawson struggled to catch up to the back of the pack, and by the time he was informed that race leader Max Verstappen was restarting proceedings, he was still over 20 seconds behind.

"It's f****** pointless, dude," Lawson said on team radio. "Oh my god. I cannot believe that." The best the Kiwi could do was P14 - having been bumped up a place by Isack Hadjar's 10-second penalty.

"Thank you for all you guys for putting my car together so quickly, I appreciate it," Lawson said on the post-race radio. "I'm sorry I couldn't get a result for everyone today, I really f****** tried, though."

Speaking to media, including GPblog, Lawson elaborated on his frustrations over the timing of the Safety Car, questioning why it took so long for him to be able to unlap himself. The Safety Car period was also extended after a bizarre spin for Lawson's teammate Arvid Lindblad.

"The thing is I got it back, and we actually probably could've still had a really good race," Lawson began. "I don't know why they they took four laps to release me. I get that when there's marshals on track and there's safety around it. I can understand that, but it's still a double yellow.

"I can still slow down for the double yellow zone and follow a delta. There's nothing stopping me. You know, I'm not gonna blow through that zone at high speeds, but it just means I can do the whole lap and catch back up.

"And, unfortunately, they waited four laps, and then they gave me half a lap to catch back up. I restarted more than 25 seconds behind the field.'

All the more frustrating for Lawson was the fact Racing Bulls showed impressive pace, with Lindblad fighting at the front before his Safety Car spin.

"So frustrating, man. I was watching the screens the whole race and I could see how close it was bunched up. At the end the race, we were very, very quick today. It's really frustrating to not have the chance to fight there."

Lawson later added: "I think Arvid was at the front. I don't know what happened, but he was fighting at the front as well. So for us to not score points just really sucks at a track where we're really fast."

#2791. Anthropic was supposed to save the AI trade. What went wrong?

Anthropic's historic IPO — the largest in U.S. history — delivered a big win for the company and its investors. But it failed at another task: reviving animal spirits in the AI trade.

The Nasdaq Composite closed down 2.25% amid a broader market pullback with the S&P 500 declining 2% to 7,175.

Investors had been looking forward to the Anthropic IPO as a "clearing event" — reviving the tech sector after months of choppy trading, the Nvidia espionage scandal, and growing concerns that AI chip sales to Asia are likely to slump as Washington cracks down on potential violations of export controls.

Some of those worries resurfaced Thursday as a short seller, Hunterbrook Media, alleged in a report that firmware sold by chipmaker AMD had turned up in U.S.-blacklisted companies. AMD stock closed down more than 7%. The chipmaker told CNBC it complies with U.S. law.

Adding to the worries, the House Foreign Affairs Committee released documents showing that a subagency of the Commerce Department approved the sale of more than 879,000 AI chips to Chinese companies. That also raised the prospect of an Asia sales slowdown under stricter export controls.

Many investors and analysts continue to express confidence in the AI trade, noting that Anthropic's successful market debut — the stock closed up 17% from its IPO price and raised more than $81 billion — bodes well for capital spending across tech.

But investors acknowledged that this is a high-tension moment and said it's time to be more strategic about the trade.

Dan Niles, founder of Niles Investment Management, described himself as a "circumspect bull," making the comparison to market conditions in 2022 when the big indices tanked in the back half of the year.

"You could have still made money that year. You just needed to have the right combination of longs and shorts," he said.

Part of the challenge, Niles said, has been a price war between the two cutting-edge frontier labs, Anthropic and OpenAI. That has posed a threat to the overall revenue and profit potential of AI and the capex going into it. Fierce competition on price is producing collective losses between the two top companies, he notes.

"The combined revenue growth for OpenAI plus Anthropic went from growing 75% sequentially in Q2 to 48% in Q3," he told CNBC. "The combined losses went from $7.9 billion to about $8.4 billion from Q2 to Q3."

While uncertainty is high now, some resolution may be on the horizon if a greater degree of commercial and regulatory clarity emerges over the next few months.

Trading experts also told CNBC that the tech sector's troubles Thursday were a more serious matter than Anthropic simply siphoning capital from other parts of the market.

"There's a reset of AI in general," said JJ Kinahan, senior vice president at Cboe Global Markets.

Investors for years have profited in tech by buying the dip, he said, but "there's been an attitude change that [you] can't buy things blindly and they'll all go up." Nvidia's ongoing declines have shaken confidence in the buy-the-dip strategy, he said.

Anthropic's next few trading days will be a key gauge of the market's strength. Friday will be a "wild card," Kinahan said. It could be a "final flush out" for the market, or a bounce that leaves the market in "no man's land" while volatility remains elevated.

A better gauge may be Monday, when options on Anthropic start trading, providing insight into whether retail investors will back the company in force — providing more of a vote of confidence in the AI trade.

"If people think we're going up [on Anthropic] they'll buy stock, but retail particularly will just buy calls," he said. "There's a bigger test coming Monday."

For now, investors continue to sell AI-related stocks.

Neoclouds, the generally highly indebted AI-dedicated computing providers at the outer edges of the AI trade, were down substantially Thursday, with CoreWeave closing down more than 6%.

Hyperscalers such as Alphabet and Amazon.com, viewed as relatively defensive AI bets, also finished lower.

One bright spot: Cybersecurity stocks continued to rally, with Palo Alto Networks and CrowdStrike both closing up. AI is finding security holes across software infrastructure, creating a scramble to patch the vulnerabilities before hackers can exploit them.

— CNBC's Daren Fonda contributed reporting.

#2789. The 76ers are fun again, so it's the return of Roob's 76ers Stats!

The 76ers are fun again, so it's the return of Roob's 76ers Stats! originally appeared on NBC Sports Philadelphia

This 76ers team is a heck of a lot of fun to watch and fun basketball means fun stats!

At 13-3, the 76ers are exactly a month into the 5th-best start in franchise history and best since they were 14-2 in 2000-01. Which turned out to be a pretty good year. They were 15-1 in 1949-50 and 1966-67 and 14-2 in 1981-82.

To help everybody get through this interminable two-day gap between 76ers games, here's our first 2026-27 installment of Roob's 76ers Stats, with a tip of the hat to Stathead.

→ The 76ers have been out-rebounded by 34 through 16 games (719-685) and their 13-3 record matches the best in NBA history by a team that's been out-rebounded after 16 games. Three other teams have been 13-3 despite getting out-rebounded, most recently the 2013-14 Miami Heat. The others are the 1979-80 Bucks and 2012-2013 Spurs. The 76ers are an NBA-best 6-3 this year when they've been out-rebounded (which means they're 7-0 when they have a rebounding edge).

→ In the win over Orlando Thursday, V.J. Edgecombe had 24 points, eight rebounds, six assists and two steals, and Joel Embiid had 26 points, six rebounds, seven assists and two steals. It was the first time two 76ers had at least 24 points, six rebounds and assists and two steals in a game in 53 years. In a win over the Celtics at the Spectrum in 1973, Fred Carter had 31 points, six rebounds, six assists and seven steals and George McGinniss had 28 points, 16 rebounds, eight assists and three steals.

→ Kentavious Caldwell-Pope, a career 37 percent shooter from 3, is shooting 44 percent so far. Anfernee Simons, a career 38 percent shooter from 3, is shooting 41 percent. Jabari Walker, a career 33 percent 3-point shooter, is at 42 percent. Dean Wade, a career 37 percent shooter, is at 41 percent. Jaylen Brown, a career 36 percent shooter from range, is at 39 percent. The 76ers are shooting just 36 percent as a team from the stripe because James and Embiid have been so bad, but everybody else is off to career-best starts from 3-point range.

→ The 76ers are 7-1 on the road, equalling their best road record ever after eight games. They were also 7-1 in 1980-81, 1981-82 and 1982-83. The 1981 team had the best road start in franchise history, going 12-1 before losing its second road game.

→ With 15.7 points, 6.8 assists and 6.8 rebounds per game, LeBron James is only the fifth 76er ever to average at least 15 ½ points and 6 ½ assists and rebounds through 16 games. The others are some familiar names: Wilt Chamberlain in 1967, Charles Barkley in 1987, Ben Simmons in 2018 and James Harden in 2023.

→ The 76ers already have more wins this year after trailing by 10 points through the first quarter than all but two full seasons since 1950. They had four in 1985-86 and again in 2022-23. The Hawks also have three wins this year when trailing by double digits after the first quarter, but they're 3-4 and the 76ers are 3-2. So they actually have a winning record when they're down by double digits going into the second quarter.

→ With Jaylen Brown (22.6, 6.6), Joel Embiid (20.0, 8.3), LeBron James (15.7, 6.8) and V.J. Edgecombe (13.6, 6.5), the 76ers are the only NBA team with four players averaging 6 ½ rebounds and 13 points. The last time they had four players in a season who averaged 13 points and 6 ½ rebounds was 1961-62 when they were the Syracuse Nationals, with Dave Gambee (16.7, 7.9), Hal Greer (22.8, 7.4), Red Kerr (16.3, 14.7), Dolph Schayes (14.7, 7.8) and Lee Shaffer (16.9, 6.8).

→ Edgecombe's 6.5 rebounds per game are 2nd-most among shooting guards behind only the Grizzlies' Cedric Coward, who is at 8.5. Edgecombe's 6.5 is also 2nd-highest ever by a 76ers shooting guard behind only Greer's 7.4 during that 1961-62 season. Edgecombe averaged 5.6 rebounds as a rookie and his 5.8 career average is highest by a shooting guard who spent more than one year with the 76ers. Greer is second at 5.3. Among all NBA players who've played at least 90 games, Edgecombe's 5.8 average is 11th-highest all-time among shooting guards (although Stathead includes some swing guard-forwards in their data).

→ Joel Embiid's 14.6 shooting percentage from 3 is worst in NBA history after 16 games (minimum 40 attempts). Embiid is 6-for-41 from the stripe this year. The previous 3-point shooting low 16 games into a season belonged to Cedric Ceballos of the Lakers in 1995-96 and the 76ers' Evan Turner in 2013-14. Both were 6-for-43 for 16.3 percent.

→ LeBron James is at 22.2 percent from 3 on 10-for-45. The Sixers are the first NBA team in 12 years with two players under 23 percent after 16 games with a minimum of 40 attempts. In 2014 Tony Wroten was 10-for-50 (10 percent) and Turner was 6-for-43 (16.3 percent).

#2787. Bills’ Week 11 win never in doubt, but they must play better vs. tougher teams

The Buffalo Bills (9-1) beat the Miami Dolphins (1-9) at Highmark Stadium, their sixth straight win and the third game in a row without allowing a touchdown. Buffalo finished with 405 total yards to Miami's 185.

Here are my notes from the game, taken as it happened.

Bills get the ball to start the game again (10th straight game opening with the possession). Start with their usual run first/PA offense. They certainly believe they came to impose their will today.

Several play-action rollouts from Josh Allen in this first drive. The run game has been contained so far (Buffalo finished with 95 rushing yards on 27 carries, 3.5 yards per carry).

That run from James Cook from the FB position is a good addition to the playbook. Tough play to stop. Blocks in the interior remind me of the way they block for the tush push (Cook: 22 carries, 89 yards, 1 TD).

Josh Allen is sacked on 3rd down. Free rusher from inside, really? Tyler Bass' FG is good, 3-0 Bills (Allen was sacked 3 times in the game).

Bills defense starts hot again, containing the Dolphins' run game and leaving the field on 3rd down. Davison Igbinosun is playing great ball at the moment (Miami punted on every first-quarter drive).

Another offensive drive and the heavy PA game continues. However, players aren't getting open downfield, and Josh is forcing it.

A fake punt pass by Tommy Doman, great completion for a 1st down. Keleki Latu with the reception. From their own 20, I don't like it. Unnecessary and very risky (30-yard completion; the Bills never punted all game).

Josh forces a deep pass outside and flirts with an INT. I like it, though. A high pass to Keon Coleman? It's supposed to be a bigger chance of a big play than an INT.

Another FG with the run game not working and the pass catchers not being able to create separation. Bills 6-0 (Bass: 54-yard FG).

The defense continues to play really well. Another impressive drive. Got lucky on 3rd down, though. Chris Bell has to catch that perfect pass by Kyle McCord, deep. Damar Hamlin and Cole Bishop clearly beat on the play, but the rookie bubbled it (McCord: 12-of-33, 102 yards, 36.4% completion rate).

Josh Allen starts to take the checkdowns available to him, and it makes all the difference in the world. The offense is moving downfield easily (Ty Johnson: 7 catches on 8 targets, 55 yards).

DJ Moore is so important. No hesitation by Josh throwing to him (Moore: 8 catches, 91 yards, 1 TD).

Josh misses Dawson Knox on a check-and-release route, already in the red zone. It would've been a walk-in TD to the veteran TE. He had nobody ahead of him.

Very next play, TD Bills. Josh makes up for the previous misfire, finds Moore on a crossing route in the back of the endzone (4-yard TD, capping a 12-play, 79-yard drive). XP is good. 13-0 Bills.

Dolphins finally can move down the field a bit, starting with great field position from their kick return. Run game could move the sticks a bit and put them in FG range. 13-3 Bills (Daniel Carlson, 52-yard FG; Miami had 6 kick returns for 159 yards).

Good to see Tre'Davious White back for some snaps. Wanna see the same with Shaq Thompson. Those vets can be valuable depth pieces late in the season or even in the playoffs.

Also great to see rookies like Kaleb Elarms-Orr and Davison Igbinosun playing better and better as the year progresses.

DJ Moore is so reliable. Great catch on 3rd and 12. 1st down.

Keon Coleman with a catch-and-run on a quick slant, getting very physical with the YAC. Love to see it.

After forcing it and almost getting intercepted, Josh quickly recognizes Ty Johnson open in the flat and delivers it, the RB does the rest. 1st and goal Buffalo.

James Cook punches it in. 20-3 Bills (1-yard TD, capping an 11-play, 57-yard drive; Cook went over 1,000 rushing yards for the season).

Another 3-and-out by the defense. The opponents they're facing are questionable for sure, but we can't deny how good they've looked recently. Deone Walker is a nightmare for IOLs, and Ed Oliver is too quick on one-on-ones. Greg Rousseau has really become a top edge rusher, and the miscommunication issues are gone. Impressive (Bills defense: 2 sacks, 8 QB hits, 5 tackles for loss, and 3 three-and-outs forced; Rousseau had 2 sacks, 10 on the season, a career high).

Great play by the Dolphins' young duo. Chop Robinson beats Spencer Brown inside and gets to Josh's face, hitting his arm as he throws. The ball goes up, and Jacob Rodriguez intercepts it. Worse than the INT is DJ Moore getting hurt on the play, with what looked like a nasty ankle sprain.

Shaq almost intercepts it on 4th down. Ball game. Bills win, 26-9.

Buffalo did its homework. A lot of margin for improvement, but I can't complain when their bad game against poor competition is a 26-9 win where they never felt in trouble during the game (Bills 9-1, matching the best 10-game start in franchise history, with five of their drives ending in field goal attempts instead of touchdowns). They need to play better against better competition, but we know they can do that.

The next stretch of this schedule should determine their seed 1 chances, starting with next Thursday's matchup against the Chiefs. After this stretch, we should have a real picture of who the 2026- 27 Buffalo Bills are and their true chances at a SB appearance.

#2786. 'I'm a running back': Versatile Michael Terry III emerges as ground threat for No. 5 Texas

During the middle of the 2025 season, the Texas football team needed someone to help it prepare for Georgia's ground game.

After weighing its options, Texas settled on Michael Terry III. Terry was listed on the roster as a wide receiver, but the then-freshman had played multiple positions in high school (he was even a punter at Alamo Heights). The 6-foot-3, 216-pound Terry was rated by both ESPN and On3 as the top athlete prospect in the 2025 recruiting cycle, so Texas decided to see if he could lead the scout team's rushing attack.

ABC; 1300 AM, 1370 AM, 98.1 FM, 105.3 FM (Spanish).

During that week, Texas coach Steve Sarkisian was approached by some of his defensive assistants with a suggestion to take a closer look at Terry's film. Sarkisian recalled being told that "this guy's a problem. He's a problem at running back." Terry later said he was just trying to have fun and beat the Longhorns' first-team defense during that scout team stint, but his effort was still remembered by his teammates a year later.

"Well, let me tell you my shoulder was hurting," Texas defensive lineman Hero Kanu recently said.

Texas Longhorns quarterback Arch Manning (16) takes photos with fans after the Longhorns' 45-3 win over the Arkansas Razorbacks at Royal-Memorial Stadium in Austin, Nov. 21, 2026. (Sara Diggins/Austin American-Statesman)

While seated next to Kanu, safety Jelani McDonald laughed and added that "my neck was hurting, my body was getting a little sore during the week. (I told him) we've got a game on Saturday now, you can't be doing all this hard running."

So Texas began to transition Terry into the offensive backfield, which was a move that didn't immediately pay dividends. As a freshman, he played only 13 offensive snaps, according to Pro Football Focus. His lone rushing attempt came in the second quarter of the Citrus Bowl.

Recently, though, Terry has had a bigger impact as No. 5 Texas (9-2, 6-2 SEC) has attempted to restock its running backs depth following a season-ending injury to Hollywood Smothers. Terry has gotten at least four offensive touches in each of UT's last five games. And during Saturday's 45-3 win over Arkansas, he capped the first drive of the second half with a 1-yard touchdown run.

"Losing Hollywood was tough on us, but the byproduct of that has been Michael Terry's continued to raise his game," Sarkisian said.

For the season, Terry has rushed 36 times for 170 yards and two touchdowns. And as he earned more playing time this month, Texas found a variety of ways to use him.

Texas Longhorns head coach Steve Sarkisian takes in the field as the team warms up ahead of the Longhorns' game against the Arkansas Razorbacks at Royal-Memorial Stadium in Austin, Nov. 21, 2026. (Sara Diggins/Austin American-Statesman)

At Missouri on Nov. 7, Terry powered his way to a first down on a third-and-3 attempt to keep a scoring drive alive. During the opening minutes of the LSU game on Nov. 14, Terry motioned out wide ahead of a 14-yard catch before he returned to the backfield on the next snap and ran for 3 yards. In the Arkansas game, Terry cut through a hole and ran untouched into the end zone where he hit the viral "Bend" dance in celebration.

MORE: How Texas can beat out Alabama, Tennessee to reach 2026 SEC championship

With that versatility, it may be fair to ask Terry how he now views himself. Is he a running back? An out-of-position receiver? Or is he simply an athlete?

"I say I'm a running back primarily," Terry said. "I'm still an athlete. I can still catch the ball, run the ball, throw the ball a little bit, but I say primarily I'm a running back."

While Smothers and Raleek Brown have both had their moments, Texas is averaging just 127.5 rushing yards per game. That ranks 110th nationally and 13th in the SEC. The annual statistics in the UT record book date back to the 1950 season, and none of the previous 75 seasons featured a season with a rushing average of less than 135 yards per game.

But the emergence of Terry has given Texas an additional weapon as it prepares for this week's regular-season finale at Texas A&M (4-7, 1-7). At 137.9 yards per game, A&M has the SEC's fourth-worst rushing defense. The Aggies have allowed a 100-yard rusher in each of their last two games against Texas.

#2785. Observations after Sixers come back for strong shorthanded victory

Observations after Sixers come back for strong shorthanded victory originally appeared on NBC Sports Philadelphia

The Sixers stretched their winning streak to four games and snagged a strong road win Wednesday night over the Magic.

They earned a 121-116 victory in Orlando to improve to 13-3 on the season. The Magic dropped to 6-8.

Joel Embiid had 26 points, seven assists, six rebounds, three blocks and two steals.

VJ Edgecombe posted a season-high 24 points, eight boards and six assists.

LeBron James scored 22 points and Anfernee Simons added 18.

Magic star Franz Wagner had 27 points, 11 assists and eight rebounds.

The Sixers had a long injury list in Orlando. Tyrese Maxey (right foot soreness), Jaylen Brown (left hamstring strain) and Dean Wade (left hamstring soreness/tightness) were among their players out. Sixers head coach Nick Nurse told reporters in Orlando that Brown will be re-evaluated when the team returns to Philadelphia.

Orlando was without star forward Paolo Banchero (right high ankle sprain).

The Sixers will start a three-game homestand when they face the Bucks on Saturday afternoon. Here are observations on their win over the Magic:

The Sixers' undermanned starting lineup was Edgecombe, Simons, Kentavious Caldwell-Pope, James and Embiid.

After scoring 37 points in the Sixers' win over the Nets, Embiid had a subpar start. He began 1 for 6 from the floor and committed a turnover that led to an Anthony Black dunk.

The Sixers' lineup looked exactly like a unit that hadn't built any chemistry yet. The team was not sharp early on either end of the floor.

Desmond Bane drained a long three-pointer to give Orlando a 27-12 advantage. Orlando made 11 of its first 14 field goals and led by as many as 17 points in the first quarter.

Labaron Philon Jr. and Justin Edwards joined the Sixers' rotation. So did two-way contract players Tacko Fall and Caleb Love.

Philon impressed in the first quarter, converting two floaters and a corner three. The rookie passed his previous NBA scoring high of five points and finished the night with 10 on 4-for-5 shooting.

Fall played 10 minutes and posted two points, three rebounds and three blocks. He slammed in a dunk and rejected two shots early in the second quarter. Credit to Fall for giving the Sixers a good backup center performance and doing his job as a rim-protecting ultra-big man.

Love entered in the second quarter and was ready to fire, hitting a catch-and-shoot three-pointer on his first touch.

The Sixers' zone defense also helped them eat into the Magic's lead. The zone seemed to disrupt Orlando's offensive rhythm and it was active enough to prevent the Magic from consistently creating high-quality looks.

Edgecombe's buzzer-beating mid-range jumper cut the Sixers' deficit to 69-64 after two quarters.

The Sixers pulled within a single point on multiple occasions in the third quarter, including on a Philon three late in the period. They trailed by five points after the third.

Fall continued to play well early in the fourth quarter. He deterred the Magic's drivers from getting all the way to the rim as the very large back line of the Sixers' zone defense and made eye-catching hustle plays on both sides of the ball.

The Sixers outscored Orlando by seven points on the night with Fall on the court. Soon after Embiid subbed back in, he evened the game at 104-all with two free throws. Jabari Walker swished a three to finally push the Sixers in front.

The Magic regained a late lead with a Jalen Suggs three. James then drove and kicked the ball out to Edgecombe, who nailed one of his customary clutch jumpers to put the Sixers back on top.

No matter the night-to-night personnel, the Sixers have been fantastic at the tail end of close games. They're now 11-0 in clutch situations, which the NBA defines as games within a five-point differential in the final five minutes.

#2783. Cathie Wood’s Ark Invest was a big buyer of Anthropic as she tells CNBC she’s ‘quite optimistic’

Cathie Wood was a major buyer of Anthropic following its debut Thursday on the Nasdaq, adding shares in all her major funds.

Wood's firm, which already owned private shares of the artificial intelligence company in its Ark Venture Fund leading up to the initial public offering, bought $90.8 million worth of Anthropic in six funds, according to a trade notification from the firm.

"We've owned Anthropic since early '23," Wood told CNBC's "Squawk Box" on Friday. "It's our best performing equity by far in ARKVX, our venture fund. ... Our confidence has grown with time as it really took over the enterprise" AI business.

Anthropic shares jumped 17% in their first day, but closed well off the highs of the session and were headed for sizable losses Friday. The Nasdaq, which was expected to get a boost from a major IPO reviving enthusiasm about the technology trade, fell by 2% on Thursday.

"We're climbing a wall of worry," said Wood. "Everybody is worried about AI hype and the political situation after the election, but I think we'll look back and say, OK this is more like the '80s and '90s, climbing a wall of worry you know with bricks much like what we have today: inflation, interest rates, wars, new technologies and so forth so quite optimistic here."

The Anthropic stakes are relatively small across the funds, but Wood typically will buy shares in companies she likes steadily during long stretches. Wood said she is "averaging down" with her purchases of Anthropic.

In her $6.1 billion Ark Innovation ETF, Anthropic is a 0.3% stake. The biggest holdings in that fund are Tesla and SpaceX, representing more than 21% of the fund together.

Ark Innovation, which retail investors piled into during the pandemic as it gained about 150% in 2020, tumbled the subsequent next two years, losing 67% in 2022 alone. Wood's following was dented after the big losses.

The fund had a comeback stretch as the bull market revved back up from 2023 to 2025, but has fallen on hard times again this year, down more than 10% in 2026. The S&P 500 is up 4.8% this year.

Wood dismissed comparisons of today's environment to the dot-com bubble.

"Back then if an Anthropic had gone public, it probably would have gone up threefold the first day," she said. "That's how crazy things were."
