#2780. All six Thanksgiving teams won on Sunday

The Madden movie failed to properly honor the great John Madden. The franchises that will play on Thanksgiving have done their part to make up for it.

All six of the teams that will take the field on the annual day the NFL uses to recognize and remember Madden emerged with wins in the early window on Sunday.

The Lions beat the Bucs, 31-21. The Bears suffocated the Saints, 21-13. The Eagles continued the Steelers' inability to win in Philly, 19-16. The Cowboys overwhelmed the Titans, 51-30. And the Chiefs and Bills made easy work of the Cardinals and Dolphins, respectively, 34-7 and 26-9.

It sets up a trio of consequential games on Thursday. The 8-2 Bears at the 8-2 Lions could be the most significant early-window Thanksgiving game of all time. The Eagles-Cowboys game has some added sizzle, now that the Cowboys have a chance to get to .500. And the 8-2 Chiefs at the 9-1 Bills should feel like a playoff game.

With all six teams winning on Sunday, everybody wins on Thursday.

And the game between the 6-4 Packers and the 6-4 Rams the night before the tripleheader should be a good one, too. Not to mention the Black Friday game between the likely 8-2 Broncos and the suddenly desperate 6-4 Steelers.

#2779. Steve Kerr, coaching staff tasked with burden of lifting impaired Warriors

Steve Kerr, coaching staff tasked with burden of lifting impaired Warriors originally appeared on NBC Sports Bay Area

SAN FRANCISCO – After sinking from inspirational to abominable following one cataclysmic instant, the Warriors returned home this week hoping to find their way back to mediocrity.

That, after all, was the reasonable expectation in the absence of Stephen Curry, whose oblique strain sustained 11 days ago left the Warriors, then 8-3, without their physical nerve center and emotional support sniper.

If Curry's teammates could win 50 percent of their games without him, that would defy history; the Warriors have lost 63 percent of their games when he is sidelined. They were 7-2 this season with him before the injury – splitting two games on his mandated rest nights – and they are 0-4 without him.

These were not ordinary losses but public spankings.

Until Curry returns, and there is no timeline, the burden of being competitive will get heavier on the heads of coach Steve Kerr and his staff, along with the primary players on the roster.

Can the Warriors get greater impact from veterans like Draymond Green, De'Anthony Melton and reserves Al Horford and Georges Niang? Can youngsters Yaxel Lendeborg and Brandin Podziemski, who rank behind only Curry in offensive production, recover from a forgettable road trip?

"The game is obviously harder for everybody without Steph, but especially the guys who have to take on the ballhandling and scoring role," Kerr said Saturday after practice. "I don't love that conversation, particularly for those two guys. That with Steph out, they have to do this, they have to do that. We have to do this, or we have to do that.

"If our execution isn't good, we can't expect them to make something out of nothing. If we can execute better . . . and that's what today's practice was about, and that's what the last couple of days have been about for our staff. Understanding why we're not executing better and giving our guys something to hang their hats on offensively and that's our job. That's what we've been working on."

Can't it be difficult, particularly for young players with expectations, to avoid the pitfall of believing they must fill some of the void created by a missing superstar?

"It's challenging," Podziemski conceded. "It's human nature. It's ego. It's pride. You want to showcase what you can do. That's for everybody."

Podziemski struggled to find his footing, and Lendeborg spent the entire road trip out of sorts, as if the inner "dawg" that was so visible through the first 11 games had gone astray. Aside from a 20-point second half in a 21-point loss to the sorry New Orleans Pelicans, he rarely was a presence. He clearly was affected by his inability to excel in an elevated role.

"That's why I'm (Yaxel's) coach, and that's why I talked to him today," Kerr said. "He's a rookie and he's three weeks (four weeks) into this thing. He's learning and he's growing. And it's my job to help him."

Lendeborg's intricate path to the NBA required considerable mental fortitude, which suggests he'll probably restart his engine and get back to hooping, maybe as soon as Sunday against the impressive Boston Celtics.

But navigating such circumstances while chasing potential can be tricky.

"When your best player is out, I think there's a natural instinct to look inward and say what do I need to do more of, for everybody one through 15," Podziemski said. "But when you think about it that way, it harms the team more than helps because you're putting yourself before the team. If you just think about what the team needs, no matter if Steph is in or out, whatever you're good at just bring that to the table."

Losing five consecutive games following an eight-game win streak has the entire operation trying to fix what's broken. There's no suitable replacement for what Curry brings, so it's about limiting mistakes – and there have been plenty on offense and defense – and the Warriors reminding opponents they came to play.

Not for a few possessions or even a full quarter. For all 48 minutes.

"There's no magic pill to it," Kerr said. "It's everything. It's a comprehensive game. If our defense improves, our offense will improve. But it has to start on the defensive end. We can't sit here and say we need to replace Steph's scoring, or we need to throw the ball to so-and-so. It's defense, defense, defense. Grit, competitiveness, fighting.

"Our offense will come from that. We will make some tweaks, no question, but it is it's not about the tweaks. It's about the competitive fire."

That's the surest way for the Warriors to be competitive with Curry, and it's the only way they can compete without him. Sure, they opened the season generally projected to hover around .500. Mediocrity until Jimmy Butler and Moses Moody return when the calendar flips to 2027.

Golden State's eight-game win streak raised the bar, as the Warriors looked like a playoff-caliber team. The ensuing five-game losing streak lowered the bar, as they were like one of the worst teams in the league.

As much as Curry means to this team, it has enough to climb out of the mudhole that formed with five consecutive losses an average of 23.2 points. And, yes, the rise must start with defense.

#2777. 'We're not going to talk about schematics': Cal PR blocks QB from answering question

Tosh Lupoi's debut season in Berkeley has supplied plenty of oddities, but even by those standards, Saturday's big game was a two-part spectacle, with a perplexing quarterback plan on the field followed by an even more perplexing intervention at the podium.

Long before the postgame theatrics, Cal's offense had onlookers scratching their heads throughout a 44-28 loss to Stanford. Star quarterback Jaron-Keawe Sagapolutele was calling plays in the huddle before trotting out to line up at receiver, running back Adam Mohammed was taking direct snaps, and third-string quarterback Dominic Ingrassia was frequently spelling the sophomore under center.

When the broadcast asked Lupoi to explain the strategy, he offered about as little as a head coach possibly could.

"We're just doing everything we can to win the game," Lupoi said.

Lupoi was considerably more expansive after the game, but not necessarily any clearer. Over several meandering minutes, the first-year coach explained that Sagapolutele had been hobbled by a high-low hit in Cal's previous game, couldn't practice early in the week, and threw a grand total of two passes on Wednesday, when what Lupoi called "the monsoon" rolled through Berkeley.

By Thursday, Sagapolutele was at roughly 50%, so the staff settled on a contingency plan that kept him out of harm's way and only opened up the passing game if the weather allowed it. That's why Sagapolutele threw more once the rain eased up in the second half.

"The guy's an absolute warrior, so I'm thankful, didn't want to put him in any harm's way," Lupoi said.

When a reporter pointed out that Sagapolutele apparently wasn't healthy enough to start the game but was fine to play once the rain let up, Lupoi insisted his quarterback did, in fact, start, noting he was on the field for the first play. That was technically true, provided you count lining up at receiver.

That made Ingrassia the natural candidate to shed some light on how the arrangement looked from inside the huddle. He never got the opportunity, however, as a Cal staffer cut in after a reporter asked about Sagapolutele's role in the offense.

"We're not going to talk about schematics, next question," the staffer said.

That's a curious line to draw in the sand, considering Lupoi had just spent several minutes at that same podium describing the injury, the waterlogged practice week, and the weather-dependent game plan in exhaustive, if somewhat rambling, detail. Whatever schematic secrets Cal was so intent on guarding, its head coach had already spilled most of them, and anyone who watched Cal's offense for four quarters had seen the rest.

Somebody in Cal's communications department might want to brush up on the Streisand effect.

#2775. Open Thread: Spurs air “The Blueprint” honoring Harrison Barnes leadership and dedication

I was fortunate enough to attend the Emirates Cup game between the San Antonio Spurs and the Golden State Warriors. Not only was thirty-seven point drubbing a sight to behold, the energy in the arena was palpable. But there is something to watching the games from the comfort of my own couch. One of those is the commentary, another is the replays, and finally, there are the features.

When I finally rewatched the game on DAZN, I enjoyed this video presentation in the first act of the halftime break:

"The Blueprint" is a three-minute tribute honoring the Spurs veteran Harrison Barnes. Barnes originally came to San Antonio in a three-team trade involving the Spurs, Sacramento Kings, and Chicago Bulls. The trade sent DeMar DeRozan to Sacramento and included RaiQuan Gray, Chris Duarte, and led to Charles Bassey being waived.

Since arriving in San Antonio, Barnes has taken on a leadership role and offered a solid veteran presence to the fledgling Spurs team. He is a 2015 NBA Champion through his time with the Golden State Warriors. On the court, he's known for his consistent 3-point shooting and high basketball IQ. In the locker room, he exhibits an example of maturity and patience that lifted the team's spirits as they put the pieces together.

Away from the game, he —along with his with Brittany— have time and again displayed grace and generosity in the community. Through multiple philanthropic endeavors, the Barnes have contributed to projects involving improvements to recreational locales, helping local students afford college tuition, and treating patrons to groceries during the holidays.

#2774. Beaks & Geeks: Cardinals, Chiefs Winners & Losers: Arizona taken to the woodshed – again

The Arizona Cardinals are on the road against the Kansas City Chiefs, who are one of the best teams in the AFC as well as in the league. They have a great offense. Perhaps they are looking beyond the matchup against the Cardinals since they are playing the one-loss Buffalo Bills on Thanksgiving Day.

The Cardinals came into this game starting their fourth quarterback of the young season. The NFL record is five, so, there's that.

Arizona At the final gun, the Cardinals were never in the game as they were beaten pretty badly in the first half, then allowed the Chiefs to coast to a 34-7 win.

So, who played well for the Cardinals? Who didn't?

LB Jack Gibbens – Mr. Dependable for the Cardinals had another good day along the defense as he led all defenders with 10 total tackles. Stopped RB Emmett Johnson for just a two yard gain in the first quarter that ended up being three points instead of six. As WR Xavier Worthy was rumbling downfield in the third quarter, Gibbens came from behind to tackle the receiver after a 20-yard gain that could have been a touchdown. On a third-and-five with 2:31 left in the game, Gibbens nailed RB Nate Carter for just a two-yard gain, which forced a punt.

P Blake Gillikin – Probably the offense's MVP today. Had three punts downed inside the 20 with one on the three and another at the one yard line. Six punts for 241 yards with a 40.2 average and a long of 55 yards. He was more focused on pinning the Chiefs deep than his net average. Nice work.

WR Harrison Wallace III – Somebody has to be listed as having a great game, so here is the undrafted rookie playing well despite not being named the starter and coming up with good plays. In the first quarter with Arizona driving on a third-six, Wallace turned a short pass into a 25-yard gain to the KC 26-yard line. He got 10 yards in the third quarter and then on the same drive facing a third-and-five, he caught the short pass to the right for 10 more yards to keep the drive going. Led all receivers with 7 targets, 4 receptions for 57 yards, and a 14.3 yards per catch average. Nice day at the office.

Third-and-two stop – It looked like Kansas City was going to run away with this game when they faced a third-and-two with 7:36 left in Quarter 3. The ball was handed to RB Emmett Johnson who was stuffed by DTs Walter Nolen and Roy Lopez for no gain that led to a punt.

Second drive of the game – KR Max Melton got a decent return to set up Arizona at the Cardinals' 37. Two short passes gained a first down. Then Wallace hauled in the 25-yarder to the Chiefs' 48. A sack, then two short passes and two short runs placed the ball at the KC eight yard line as TE Trey McBride converted a third-and-five by gaining six. QB Josh Johnson found WR Simi Fehoko crossing in the end zone for six. The score was now 14-7 Kansas City. It just felt good at that moment, didn't it?

Covering #87 – With TE Travis Kelce being the Chiefs main pass catcher and a fav target of QB Patrick Mahomes, it would make sense for DC Nick Rallis to come up with a great gameplan to eliminate the talented tight end from the game, right? So, whose responsibility was to cover Kelce? Well, nobody.

And nobody covered him all game. That was the gameplan? Passes to Kelce moved the chains all first half, not to mention he scored two touchdowns, both he was wide open. On his first touchdown in the first quarter, LB Mack Wilson was trailing Kelce the entire time so all Mahomes had to do was zip it in there. With 2:27 left in the first quarter, Kelce gained 13 yards but was not covered. He caught a ball that got 14 early in the second quarter, and was tackled after getting good running yardage but no coverage on the pass. 13 yards on a TE screen. Another TD catch that he was open in the second quarter. Kelce was not challenged all day, and chipped, then released and found open areas all game long. 7 targets, 6 catches for 74 yards and two touchdowns.

CB Kei'Trel Clark – With 7:14 remaining before the half, Clark played well off WR Josh Downs, then bit on the arm cock of Mahomes. Downs then simply ran past Clark for a 26-yard gain. In the first quarter, Clark dropped a sure interception. He gave up nine yards in the second quarter to RB Emmett Johnson by yet again, giving a large cushion and 13-yards to WR Josh Downs on a third-and-10. That drive ended in another Chiefs TD. Five total tackles, mostly from chasing down the receiver after he made a large gain.

Offense first half – The Chiefs had 152 yards on offense in the first quarter and 295 at the half. The Cardinals had 13 yards on offense in the first quarter and 78 yards in the first half. 21 first downs for KC, five for Arizona. Nine rushing yards for the Cardinals whereas the Chiefs had 69. Trey McBride three catches for 17 yards. Time of possession: Kansas City – 20:06, Arizona – 9:54. Score: 27-7. Yeah, all of this in just two quarters.

Fourth and one play – As the second quarter was winding down, the Cardinals had a third-and-2 at their own 36 yard line behind just 17-7. A Bam Knight run made just one yard. Run out the punt team, right? Their own 37 yard line, correct? Down just 10 points, correcto? That would be a negative. Instead, a jet sweep by WR Simi Fehoko was ordered who gained nothing. It was Fehoko's first carry of the year by the way. Turnover of downs, and a subsequent Chiefs TD in just three downs that moved the scoreboard to 24-7. One word: completely dumb. Okay, that's two words.

Best quote of the game – As the Chiefs moved up and down the field and scored on all five possessions in the first half, TV announcer Ian Eagle stated, "The Chiefs are doing whatever they want."

RT Josh Fryar – The backup tackle had no answer for #56, DE George Karlaftis, who was in the offensive backfield all game it seemed. Johnson was sacked three times, as Karlaftis had 2.5 sacks, 4 tackles, 2 tackles for loss, and a whopping 5 QB hits. Fryar just could not stop the outside speed of Karlaftis. His sack with 11:08 left in the third quarter killed a good drive which ended in yet another Gillikin punt.

Last three games – The Cardinals offensive production in the final three games: 3 points, 6 points, and 7 points. With the escalation, does that mean this offense is improving?

CB/KR Max Melton – Had two good kickoff returns, one for 37 yards while the other was for 36 yards.

Chiefs RB Emmett Johnson – At Nebraska, Johnson was a First Team All-American and was named the "Big 10 RB of the Year" plus voted First Team All-Big 10. He was taken in the fifth round. Instead of taking an elite pass rusher with the third overall pick, or an offensive tackle, the Cardinals took Notre Dame RB Jeremiyah Love, who is on IR with repetitive ankle issues and has played sparingly. Johnson just ran for his second consecutive 100-yard game where he scored in both contests and has a rushing touchdowns in four consecutive games.

DE Josh Sweat – Was very active with pass rush although the only stats he registered was two QB hits. The Walter Nolen sack in the third quarter was because Sweat forced Mahomes out on a third-and-six and into the waiting arms of Nolen.

WR Simi Fehoko – Okay, he had the game's only points with the TD catch in the first quarter, but he also had several bad plays. On the fourth down run in Cardinals territory, he never any attempt to go forward and was stopped for no gain. Then with 6:48 left in the third quarter, he dropped a pass that would have converted into a first down into KC territory. Had five targets, yet only caught one?

QB Josh Johnson – Okay, he had one day of practice. We get it. Maybe the offensive coaching staff should have placed tape on the front of the receivers and tight ends helmets with their name on it so that the new guy could at least call them by name. He had his moments, and had some decent pockets, but was also pestered a lot with a bad offensive line. 18 for 31 for 155 yards, a 5.0 average per reception, sacked three times with an 82.1 QB rating.

#2773. As Anthropic shares tank, investors scramble to hedge. It could be very expensive

As Anthropic shares tumble toward their IPO price of $83, investors are looking for ways to hedge. Unfortunately, they may have to wait until Monday, when options on the AI giant are expected to list.

If Anthropic breaks its IPO price, it will represent a 27% decline from Thursday's intraday high of $113. The wild price swings offer clues on how expensive it might be to buy protection on the new tech giant once options begin trading.A 20% intraday move points to an implied volatility somewhere between 100 and 150 for Anthropic options, according to estimates from single-stock market-makers on the Cboe trading floor in Chicago.

Options traders are also looking at how SpaceX traded in the days following its offering for clues on how options might price.

Implied volatility in SpaceX started at 116, but that didn't deter options traders big and small from piling into the contracts tracking the stock, with about 1.7 million contracts traded on the first day for a total options premium of about $2.5 billion. Far out-of-the-money call contracts were some of the most popular trades as the stock soared 40% in its first three sessions.One key difference between now and then? Anthropic is trading against the backdrop of S&P 500 volatility close to 30, compared to 17.3 when SpaceX debuted. "I expect ANT options to be super active," said Tom Sosnoff, co-founder of the trading platforms ThinkOrSwim and TastyTrade. "I expect heavy options volume."One thing Anthropic and SpaceX have in common is heavy retail involvement in both IPOs, with retail brokerages receiving a 20% allocation from SpaceX and reports pointing to between a 15-20% allocation from Anthropic on Thursday. "Round one of the Anthropic IPO easily goes to the individual investor," Sosnoff added. "Retail participants bailed early at the best prices of the day while institutional investors paid up to top off their underwhelming allocations. I believe retail was aggressively reloading in the mid-90s."Further signs of retail demand will become evident when options likely begin trading on Monday. One thing to watch: the spread between out-of-the-money call and put contracts, which was heavily skewed toward calls in the case of SpaceX. "I would be careful comparing Anthropic to SpaceX," said Dennis Davitt, CIO and Co-Founder of Millbank Dartmoor Portsmouth. "From a market-structure perspective, the two are dramatically different. The availability and cost of borrowing will be different, the companies are at very different stages of maturity, and SpaceX has allowed employees to monetize portions of their holdings over time. All of those factors matter when pricing options."

#2771. How Daniil Medvedev’s ranking has changed since the end of 2025 as he concludes 2026 campaign

Daniil Medvedev has enjoyed an excellent campaign on the ATP circuit.

Medvedev has bounced back emphatically from a poor 2025 season, winning multiple titles and producing some of his best tennis when the stakes have been highest.

The Russian's good season was encapsulated in Turin over the past week; Medvedev advanced to the semi-finals, beating Flavio Cobolli and Frances Tiafoe – before losing to a rampant Alexander Zverev.

Following the conclusion of Medvedev's season, it is worth assessing just how much Medvedev has progressed over the past year.

Medvedev's 2025 season was littered with poor results and disappointing performances.

The Russian star won just one match at Grand Slam events throughout the entire year and captured just one title – the Almaty Open.

Medvedev was ranked fifth on the ATP Tour at the start of 2025. After 12 months, his ranking had fallen to 13.

However, Medvedev refused to let one poor season define his future campaigns, utilising all of his experience to bounce back in 2026.

Medvedev has performed at a much higher level throughout the 2026 campaign, winning four ATP titles and reaching at least the semi-finals at three Masters events.

However, the Russian has continued to struggle on the major stage, failing to advance past the fourth round at any of the Grand Slam events.

Australian Open: Fourth round [lost to Learner Tien, 4-6, 0-6, 3-6]

Roland Garros: First round [lost to Adam Walton, 2-6, 6-1, 1-6, 6-1, 4-6]

Wimbledon: Third round [lost to Jan-Lennard Struff, 6-7, 6-7, 5-7]

US Open: Fourth round [lost to Frances Tiafoe, 6-7, 4-6, 6-7]

Despite these struggles, Medvedev's ranking has risen consistently throughout the year. As per the ATP live rankings, Medvedev will end 2026 ranked fifth in the world once again.

Medvedev is set to begin his 2027 ATP campaign at the Brisbane International in Australia.

Medvedev is the defending champion in Brisbane. He defeated Brandon Nakashima in the 2026 final, 6-2, 7-6.

"I want to dedicate this to my family," Medvedev said during his winner's speech. "They couldn't make it to Australia, it is a bit too far guys, about 23 hours from where I live and with two young kids it is not easy.

"I decided to play this tournament and we thought we would be coming all together but we stuck to the decision and my daughter had a birthday when I was here, so I dedicate it to her first birthday. This [trophy] is a bit too heavy for her though."

Medvedev is set to join Alex de Minaur and Alexander Zverev at the event. On the women's side, Elena Rybakina, Iga Swiatek and Naomi Osaka have confirmed their participation.

#2770. Wolves 104, Grizzlies 95: Defense Downs the Bear

Basketball is a funny thing. One night, the world is obsessed with your offense-capping defensive stalwart and the next you're holding the number one offense in all of basketball to 95 points while that aforementioned rim protector has an incredible game (and still missed some layups).

In a game in which they looked dominant throughout, the Minnesota Timberwolves taught the Memphis Grizzlies that there is joy in being a young, upstart team, but it is still incredibly hard to face off with a capable team on a random Saturday with some of your best players absent.

No, luckily, the Wolves have seemingly learned this lesson.

Minnesota's nine-point margin is significantly smaller than it likely should be after the Grizzlies outscored them by 12 in the fourth quarter. Don't be fooled, this was functionally a wire to wire victory.

The Wolves employed a bit of a platoon plan on offense tonight, spreading usage to each of the starters and allowing Jonathan Kuminga, Jaden McDaniels, and Ayo Dosunmu to all turn in impressive point totals. This Wolves team just keeps showing up and playing well in a way that could not be further from what Wolves fans are used to. Memphis leaned on Cedric Coward and Most Improved Player frontrunner Ty Jerome, but struggled to do much of anything until a garbage time run.

This was a game that put the floor of the Wolves on display more than its ceiling. This was a close win against an admittedly great team with clear weaknesses. If we're going to start there, with the two-in-one ceiling capper and floor raiser, there's only one man to talk about.

Look, the discourse surrounding Rudy Gobert does make sense. He may be the single greatest floor-raising defensive presence since Tim Duncan or Hakeem Olajuwon, but he also makes those two look like Moses combined with prime Escalade on offense.

(And yes, folks, going from being a bad catcher of the basketball to a stone-handed anomaly who is putting up 40ish% on layups is, in fact, a major regression).

Tonight was one of those nights where everything looked decent for Gobert, even if he only scored seven points. Considering the state of the timeline over the past few days – if you are lobbying for Myles Turner, you live in a 2K fanfiction – it was nice to have a reminder of what a *good* Gobert game can look like.

Anytime Minnesota has a great defensive game, everyone will place the flowers at Gobert's feet. This is, after all, exactly what he was supposed to do here. However, the hand-wave of "Gobert was great" doesn't give him his full deserved credit. Memphis did not look at the rim all game. That meant that by the time they got hot, they were in two deep of a hole to get out of.

The man may be an absolute deadbeat, have the health/food knowledge of a yule log (maybe one that's worm-eaten too), but he is an absurdly game-altering talent.

It may be impossible to replace him, and no one seems willing to give up anything of value for him. At that point, it seems the future holds a lot of floor-raising. After tonight, that sounds pretty perfect.

Five different players hit double digits for Minnesota tonight. Jaden McDaniels (25 points on 11/17 from the field), Ayo Dosunmu (19 points), Jonathan Kuminga (16 points on 6/9 shooting), LaMelo Ball (on 50% from the field after a bad stretch), and TJ Shannon Jr. each scored over 10 points, and together they provided all but 17 points in this game.

The headliner is a hyper-efficient Jaden McDaniels game, but it feels like he is destined to never truly empty the clip, instead opting to be a capable third option no matter who he is sharing the court with. Some days, he's the number three guy to Anthony Edwards and LaMelo Ball; other days, he's letting Greg Monroe initiate the offense while on a 10-day. Tonight, however, he deferred to Kuminga and Ayo as the two turned defense into offense.

However, the real storyline is TJ Shannon, who continues to rise up the depth chart (much to my personal surprise) as another change-of-pace option in attacking the rim. His 15 points in 26 minutes comes after he looked to have found some confidence and comfort in his last few games.

There was a tweet from a few weeks ago that sticks with me about this team. It said the Wolves have on of the best 1-9s in the league, but have the most garbage 10- 15s as well.

Depth is so hard to really pull off, but the Wolves have the right kind of options. Instead of playing around waiting for a loophole, Minnesota just straight up outperformed and out delivered what the Grizzlies could do off the bench.

The Wolves will face off against Julius Randle and the Brooklyn Nets Monday evening, 6:30pm CT on Peacock, as the two former allies will fight over who deserves their low ppg elbow touches. Considering all of this is about raising the floor, the 3-12 Nets probably wish they pursued Gobert instead of Randle.

#2769. NFL Week 11 Winners and Losers: Steelers have another horrible loss, and it seems obvious how their season will end

The Pittsburgh Steelers are likely going to the playoffs. But they also appear very likely to be headed toward being one-and-done, if they do make the postseason.

The Steelers have been one-and-done in the playoffs six straight times and the way they're playing lately doesn't give much confidence that they can get out of the wild-card round this time either.

Losing to the Cincinnati Bengals is excusable, though it was troubling how badly they got blown out. But nobody should be losing to this season's Philadelphia Eagles. The Eagles, who had lost seven in a row, beat the Steelers 19-16. Philadelphia was led by Joe Milton III, their third quarterback of the season. Milton didn't play well, throwing for just 141 yards, but the Steelers still couldn't outscore the Eagles.

Not only was there the loss, but the entire DK Metcalf experience somehow continued to get worse. Metcalf was getting into it with Eagles cornerback Cooper DeJean throughout the game and then poked DeJean in the eye after a play in the third quarter. That dirty act got Metcalf ejected and the NFL will certainly look into a suspension. It's not like it matters given how little production Metcalf has given Pittsburgh this season and pretty much through his entire Steelers tenure, but it still had to be infuriating for Pittsburgh to be stuck with less than 10 points and then watch its supposed No. 1 receiver walking back to the locker room after being kicked out. It is turning out to be a disastrous trade for Pittsburgh.

"This is definitely a game we expected to win … we need him in those games," Steelers head coach Mike McCarthy told the media afterward. "That's not acceptable to get thrown out of a game."

It was a miserable day overall for the Steelers. They couldn't get the run game going, so they abandoned it. Their leading rusher was Aaron Rodgers, with 29 yards. Their top two backs, Jaylen Warren and Rico Dowdle, combined for 43 yards on 14 carries. Rodgers continues to fade as he creeps up on his 43rd birthday. He has been good for his age but expecting him to continue to play at an above-average pace all season was always wishful thinking.

Rodgers had 227 yards and a touchdown, but the touchdown came late with the Steelers trailing by 10 points. That was the only time Pittsburgh got in the end zone. The rest of the season might not be pretty, and it will come with more of Rodgers ranting against everyone around him.

The defense wasn't bad, but when it needed a stop on third-and-6 in the final minutes, Milton found DeVonta Smith for the game-clinching first down. Pittsburgh also gave up a 38-yard touchdown run to Saquon Barkley that was most of Philadelphia's offense on Sunday.

Steelers fans have to be wondering how they can get off the hamster wheel. Every season seems about the same. The Steelers pull off some unlikely wins, and their record is just good enough to get into the playoffs. Then they get to the postseason and get blown out. Their last five playoff losses have come by double digits.

The Steelers had a coaching change, going from Mike Tomlin to McCarthy, and nothing has really changed. They're probably good enough to make the playoffs in a weak AFC wild-card race. And there aren't any signs the last couple weeks that they're able to go on the road and upset a team like Jacksonville or Denver in the wild-card round or even keep the game close. And perhaps the Steelers, at 6-4, could fade in the wild-card race, considering their next three games are against Denver, Houston and Jacksonville.

It was another rough Sunday for the Steelers. Being a likely playoff team should be something to get excited about, but it's hard when the endgame now seems obvious.

Here are the rest of the winners and losers from Week 11 of the NFL season:

Josh Johnson: It didn't matter if the Arizona Cardinals beat the Kansas City Chiefs on Sunday. Johnson's story was a great one either way.

Johnson got the start for the Cardinals when Carson Beck's back injury kept him out of practice during the week. The 40-year-old made some history on his first snap, as the second-oldest non-specialist to play for the Cardinals, behind only Jim Thorpe, who played one game at age 41 for the Chicago Cardinals in 1928.

And Johnson, who has played for 15 teams, threw a touchdown in the first half, the 15th of his long and strange career. Johnson was reasonable, with 155 yards and that touchdown.

Predictably, the Cardinals couldn't keep up with the Chiefs. Kansas City got another big game from rookie Emmett Johnson, who had 105 yards rushing and 38 more receiving as Kenneth Walker III got another week off to nurse a groin injury. It was no surprise the Chiefs won 34-7. But even in the loss, Johnson playing and doing OK at 40 years old was fun to see.

Bills' defense: At some point Buffalo will face a tough offense. It has had a few weeks of playing against bad offenses with backup quarterbacks. But let's give the Bills credit for handling business.

Buffalo's defense did its job Sunday, holding down the Miami Dolphins led by quarterback Kyle McCord. The Bills won 26-9, allowing just 185 yards and no touchdowns.

The Bills' defense was not very good to start the season, bringing into question whether Buffalo was good enough on that side of the ball to win a Super Bowl. The Bills won't get to see quarterbacks like Joe Fagnano, Carson Wentz and McCord in the playoffs, so we don't know for sure if their defense has turned a corner. At least it's better than struggling against those quarterbacks.

Dak Prescott and CeeDee Lamb: Most of the focus for the Dallas Cowboys this season has been on their awful defense. That defense is a shame because it is wasting another good season of Prescott's prime.

Prescott was very good in a 51-30 win over the Tennessee Titans on Sunday, as he has been most of the season. Prescott threw for 325 yards and four touchdowns including one to Lamb, who had another big game. Lamb had 11 catches for 132 yards.

The Cowboys' defense might have been slightly better Sunday, with Mike Pettine taking over play-calling from Christian Parker. But it was the type of game the Cowboys are going to have to keep winning if they want to stay in the NFC East race, with the offense scoring enough to not make the defense's struggles matter. Prescott is capable of making it happen.

Tampa Bay Buccaneers: It's not like the Buccaneers were expected to win Sunday in Detroit. But maybe that's the problem.

The Buccaneers, like everyone else in the NFC South, seem incapable of beating a good opponent. Tampa Bay hung around for a while against Detroit, but the Lions eventually wore the Bucs out in a 31-21 win. Jahmyr Gibbs continued his fantastic season for the Lions with 118 total yards and two touchdowns.

Tampa Bay could still win the division because nobody else is running away with it. But at 3-7, it's not like the Buccaneers deserve a playoff spot.

Jesse Minter: Not all of the Baltimore Ravens' struggles this season are Minter's fault. But he's not having a great first season as head coach.

Minter's defense failed again Sunday. The Panthers put up 378 yards and won 35-33. Carolina drove at the end for a go-ahead field goal and Jacoby Brissett was picked off on a deflected pass. Brissett, however, wasn't the biggest problem. He had 276 yards and a touchdown. It's that nothing else was great for the Ravens, especially the defense. It was another blown lead for the Ravens, which has been a theme of their season.

"At the end of the day we had a lead, and we didn't hold it," Minter said. "So I'll take responsibility for that."

The Ravens are a perennial contender but that status is fading. Baltimore (4-7) lost its sixth in a row.

Minter might be a good coach over time, but there has to be skepticism after Baltimore's fade this season.

Kellen Moore: Could Moore be in trouble as New Orleans Saints head coach?

The Saints lost 21-13 to the Chicago Bears, and while that is a tough opponent, it's not like New Orleans has done much to impress this season. The Saints (3-7) haven't looked good all season. Tyler Shough hasn't progressed in his second season, though he's not getting a ton of help. The Saints' hot streak late last season, after a 1-8 start, seems like a mirage due to catching a very easy part of the schedule.

Team owner Gayle Benson had some startling comments earlier this season that seemed to be critical of Moore and the front office as well. She later said she endorsed Moore. But the losses are piling up.

#2766. Mikal Bridges is finding his groove at the right time

Remember when we all said we wouldn't care about the regular season that much after the championship? That lasted long…

As the Knicks stumbled out to a 0-3 start that didn't seem to stabilize that much until this four-game winning streak that's brought them to 10-6 ahead of tonight's game against Indiana, fans didn't seem to have the patience they promised after this same group showed they can be trusted to step up when it counts.

At the center of that impatience was Mikal Bridges, who, despite putting up a second straight awesome playoff run and fully validating the trade by helping hoist a Larry O'Brien Trophy, was the common target early on as he struggled to find his groove.

Through 11 games, he was averaging just 5.9 points a game on a rough 32.9% from the field and 26.5% from deep. He'd be benched early in games where he didn't have it for Landry Shamet, he looked even more apprehensive than usual, and his defense wasn't as impactful as usual.

We've seen bad stretches from Bridges plenty over the last two years, but this was just maddening. The ring was supposed to free him; it was supposed to allow him to tap into the best of his game for extended stretches. The weight was off his shoulders.

Instead, it just seemed to get heavier? At times, it looked like the yips, basketball edition.

But this is why you exercise patience. These guys are champions; they know how to manage the highs and lows. When they look lost, they'll figure it out.

Body language is a big deal sometimes. You started to see him break out of his shell against the Pacers, where he looked for his shot early and often. When his jumper failed him, he got to the rim. He wasn't getting to the line, but he was attacking gaps and making good reads. The activity was back defensively.

He didn't seem to carry this over into Toronto a few days later, but a pep talk from Jose Alvarado might've done the trick:

Suddenly, he was everywhere defensively. A switch seemed to flip, and it looked like he was back in that Boston series. Needing a big late comeback, he set the tone defensively. Everything he did was purposeful, as he was a +15 in the overtime victory.

The offense came back around two nights later when the Knicks really needed it. Despite being -14 in 30 minutes, he put up an efficient 17 points.

The confidence to take shots like these just wasn't there a few weeks ago. He'd miss one or two threes and just stop shooting. He'd subconsciously pass up shots that had been a part of his game for years. It's pivotal that even when the ball isn't going down that a shooter keeps shooting, and we just didn't see that.

This article comes at a great time after he called game against the Blazers on Saturday night, but I think it shows that he's got his confidence back more than anything. Watch back the tape and see if you notice anything:

First, it's the fact that he consciously looks for the ball off the inbound. A big problem with the Knicks' final-possession offense is that they have one look and no backup plan. We saw that against Indiana with KAT and we see it all the time where they just screen for Jalen Brunson to get the ball and get out of the way.

Here, Bridges goes from a screener to releasing to pop up as a backup option. Then, look at this screenshot:

There are three whole seconds left, and Toumani Camara is on a hard closeout. There is nobody near Andre Drummond underneath the basket. If Bridges immediately hits him, it's an easy dunk (or is it, with how bad his finishing has been…)

In some ways, that's the correct read. A broken inbound has left the opponent scrambling and you have an easy, game-tying bucket under the basket. Maybe if he didn't make the shot, this would be discussed more.

Regardless, the confidence of Bridges matters a whole helluva lot more than winning a regular season game. This is a guy you need in April, May, and June, not November. A 26-point blown lead sucks, blowing the first 15+-point second half lead in the regular season since April 2023 sucks, but you want him taking this shot.

Maybe he just doesn't like the Blazers. That's two game-winners against them in his three years as a Knick.

Now, if only Josh Hart can figure it out too…

#2765. Carnell Tate turns failure to touch him down by contact into a 59-yard touchdown

After the Cowboys scored their fifth touchdown in five drives, Tennessee answered with another touchdown.

Receiver Carnell Tate made a catch and went down. He wasn’t touched until after his legs were off the ground. He kept running, and he didn’t stop until he got to the end zone.

There was an immediate question as to whether Tate was down when touched because his towel was on the ground. Fox rules analyst Mike Pereira explained that the towel doesn’t make the player down.

For Tate, it was the longest catch of his career. It also was the longest throw of quarterback Cam Ward’s career.

None of it matters if the Titans can’t stop the Dallas offense. Already, the Cowboys are moving again, with a 34-23 lead.

#2764. Prediction markets outlook for stocks in 2026 dims amid AI sell-off

As stocks continue their tech-driven sell-off, prediction markets traders are seeing a bleaker outlook for the remainder of the year.

On platform Kalshi, speculators place just 6% odds that the S&P 500 will cross 8,000 in 2026. On Oct. 13, those odds were as high as 72%.

Contracts ask traders if the S&P 500 will go above various levels in 2026, and they're resolved using data from S&P Dow Jones Indices.

On Friday, the Nasdaq Composite turned negative for the year as investors continued to dump tech stocks, primarily those with exposure to the artificial intelligence build-out. The Dow Jones Industrial Average and the S&P 500 fell into correction territory, too.

The S&P 500 has slashed its year-to-date gain to just over 2%.

The Stock Trader's Almanac in a Thursday note broadly agreed that the hopes for a December rally are fading, with the caveat that a "Santa rally" could still appear at the end of the year.

"The market is under pressure and more downside can't be ruled out," the note said.

The Stock Trader's Almanac sees 2026 following the year-end pattern of 2018, when the Federal Reserve was in a tightening cycle in a midterm year. The Fed is widely expected to hike rates at its December meeting.

In that scenario, the Stock Trader's Almanac thinks more churn is to come. "The low is more likely in mid- to late December than this week."

#2763. DK Metcalf ejected after two personal fouls and two receiving yards

Steelers wide receiver DK Metcalf had a miserable game today in Philadelphia, and now his day is over.

Metcalf was ejected after picking up two personal foul penalties today against the Eagles, both for taking completely unnecessary shots at Eagles players after the play was over.

Other than the ejection, Metcalf did nothing notable today. Aaron Rodgers threw him four passes, and Metcalf only caught one of them, for two yards on third-and-3. (The Steelers got stuffed on fourth-and-1 on the next play.)

It’s been a terrible season for Metcalf, who has done nothing to justify the big price the Steelers paid for him when they traded for him and signed him to a new contract last year. Metcalf’s cap hit for the Steelers will be more than $35 million next year whether he’s on the team or not. Trading for him has proven to be a big mistake.

The Steelers have had a rough day today for other reasons, including the loss of Alex Highsmith with an ankle injury. Highsmith had two sacks and a forced fumble before leaving the game, and his injury represents a big loss for the Steelers.

The score is 16-9 Eagles in the third quarter.
